In August, the CFTC ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets.

Democratic Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Jeff Merkley are demanding answers from the White House after its teleprompter operator was accused of using advance information to place bets on President Donald Trump's speeches.

The lawmakers say that the White House has failed to take "meaningful action to address ethics concerns," in a letter to White House Counsel Will Scharf.

"We are writing to request information about how the White House is responding to allegations from earlier this year that a key presidential aide enriched himself by abusing access to nonpublic information, and how this alleged incident is shaped by the White House’s lax ethics and anticorruption policies and practices," they said.

In August, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets, which try to predict specific words that public figures will use. Perez's situation is one of several in which people have used inside information to place bets on prediction markets, including an active-duty U.S. Army soldier who used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro’s capture earlier this year.

Warren is set to take on a larger role after Democrats took control of the Senate and House in last week's midterm elections. She will lead the Senate Banking Committee, where she has said she would seek to "end the current Wild West-style lack of oversight in this area, with corrupt insiders profiting." Another Democrat, Rep. Ro Khanna, who is weighing a 2028 presidential bid, called for a ban on sports prediction markets on Tuesday.

In the letter, Warren and Merkley said that the White House needs to do more than send a warning memo and take heed of what other administrations have done, including mandatory training.

"More action is required—from the President, from you, and from other White House officials—to provide the public with confidence in the integrity of the federal government under President Trump," the senators said.

Specifically, Warren and Merkley said they want information on whether other employees had "misappropriated insider knowledge to place any bets, trades, purchase contracts, or taken any similar action for personal gain," and if training is available for nonpublic information.

The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#5035. Can crypto trust firms compete with banks in US derivatives settlements?

Crypto custodians are moving into a market handling billions in daily settlements, but regulatory relief and a shift in congressional control leave questions over their long-term role.

Crypto companies are moving deeper into financial services traditionally dominated by banks, seeking a role in US derivatives settlements, where billions of dollars change hands daily.

The push comes as regulators expand the services trust companies can provide, even as traditional banking groups challenge parts of the regulatory framework governing crypto firms’ expansion into banklike activities.

A Nov. 5 decision by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) illustrates the shift, allowing derivatives clearinghouses to use trust companies for daily settlements with clearing members, a function previously restricted to banks under the agency’s regulations.

The relief could create new business opportunities for regulated crypto custodians, but its revocable nature and changes in the US political landscape raise questions about the durability of the regulatory opening.

The scale of the derivatives settlement business offers some perspective on the opportunity.

CME Group, one of the world’s largest derivatives market operators, transferred an average of $8.7 billion per business day through its clearing systems during the first half of 2026, up from $6.7 billion a day in 2025, according to regulatory filings.

Those figures represent funds transferred between clearing firms to settle gains and losses, rather than fees earned by settlement banks. They nevertheless illustrate the scale of financial flows supported by the infrastructure that trust companies could increasingly help provide.

The CFTC’s Division of Clearing and Risk issued no-action relief on Nov. 5 following an Oct. 29 request from derivatives clearinghouse Nodal Clear, allowing clearinghouses to use trust companies for daily settlements if they meet the same evaluation and monitoring requirements applied to traditional settlement banks.

The decision addresses an inconsistency between federal commodities law, which permits customer funds to be held at banks or trust companies, and CFTC Regulation 39.14, which defines settlement banks only as banks.

Nodal Clear partnered with Coinbase in 2025 to integrate USDC into US futures trading. Coinbase Custody Trust was already expected to provide daily settlement services under the arrangement, Cointelegraph has learned.

Related: CFTC expands regulatory relief for passive trading software providers

Other crypto firms have also expanded their institutional financial services. On Nov. 4, BitGo launched its Global Markets division, combining derivatives trading, financing, collateral management and settlement capabilities.

Coinbase’s national trust bank charter also took effect on Nov. 6, allowing Coinbase National Trust Company, a separate entity from Coinbase Custody Trust, to provide federally supervised digital asset custody services.

Several major crypto firms have also secured US national trust bank approvals over the past months, including Circle, Ripple, Crypto.com and Stripe-owned Bridge. The charters, granted by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), reflect the industry’s expansion into federally regulated financial services.

The expansion of specialized trust companies has prompted objections from traditional banking groups.

In October, the Independent Community Bankers of America sued the OCC, arguing that its national trust bank charter rules could allow crypto firms to conduct certain banklike activities without meeting all the requirements imposed on conventional banks.

The lawsuit reflects a broader disagreement over how specialized financial institutions should be regulated as they expand into services also provided by banks.

Cody Carbone, CEO of The Digital Chamber, told Cointelegraph that the CFTC’s relief does not put trust companies in a better or worse position than traditional banks.

Trust companies and banks already compete for services permitted under their respective charters, Carbone said, adding that the decision gives clearinghouses more options without preventing banks from retaining their business. “A trust company charter is not a regulatory shortcut,” he said.

Despite the potential opportunities, the CFTC’s decision leaves questions about how securely trust companies can establish a long-term role in derivatives settlement.

The agency issued a no-action letter rather than formally amending its regulations. The letter states that CFTC staff will not recommend enforcement action against clearinghouses using trust companies for daily settlements if specified conditions are met.

Unlike a formal rule amendment, which generally requires a public notice-and-comment process, staff no-action relief does not change the underlying regulation and can be withdrawn.

“A change in Commission leadership could withdraw the CFTC Letter. More formal Commission action, such as amending the regulation, would be necessary to avoid this potential risk,” Jordan Yeagley, a shareholder at Buchanan Ingersoll & Rooney, told Cointelegraph.

The uncertainty comes at a politically significant moment for the agency. CFTC Chair Michael Selig remains the regulator’s sole commissioner, despite a statutory structure providing for five commissioners. Selig has led the agency since December 2025, leaving four seats vacant as the regulator expands its work on digital asset markets.

The political environment is also changing following the Nov. 3 US midterm elections, in which Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress.

The incoming Congress, which takes office in January, will have greater power to scrutinize the CFTC’s regulatory agenda. The Senate also has a role in confirming nominees to fill the agency’s vacant commissioner seats, while the House and Senate Agriculture Committees oversee the regulator.

Sen. Sherrod Brown, who won Ohio’s Senate race on Nov. 3 despite more than $60 million in opposition spending from crypto-backed political action committees, could take on a prominent role on the Senate Agriculture Committee.

#5033. Nvidia Erases 2026 Gains as Republicans Threaten Jensen Huang With Subpoena

Nvidia Erases 2026 Gains as Republicans Threaten Jensen Huang With Subpoena

Nvidia shares plunged to $166.95 Tuesday afternoon, leaving the AI chipmaker down 18.42% for November and wiping out all the gains it had accumulated in 2026.

The selloff accelerated as congressional Republicans threatened to subpoena CEO Jensen Huang over his reported relationship with a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

At that price, Nvidia was roughly 10.5% below its $186.50 closing price at the end of 2025 and 31.4% below its recent $243.37 peak. November is shaping up as its worst month since September 2022, when shares lost roughly 19.6%; the company suffered an even deeper 32% crash in April of that year.

Tuesday's drop exceeded 6% in afternoon trading, after a 4.3% fall on Monday. The two-day decline was on track to erase more than $470 billion in market value. Even after the significant drop, some analysts would still view the stock as extremely overbought in the long term, with prices much higher than what would be considered safe based on technical indicators.

Just for context, even an additional 20% crash (-43% from October) would be considered healthy in an isolated technical analysis, leaving the price still above the average price of the last 50 months. Prices tend to either bounce or trade near that line. When prices go too far away from the average, that usually means there is an unusual appetite for the asset, typically driven more by FOMO than fundamentals.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast told ABC News on Tuesday that he was prepared to subpoena Huang unless the CEO responded by Wednesday, November 11, to a request for a closed-door briefing. No subpoena had been issued at the time of the report.

Mast and two other Republican committee chairs asked Huang to appear on November 18 and explain his interactions with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed. The lawmakers also want details about Nvidia's safeguards against foreign espionage, according to a letter reviewed by Reuters.

U.S. authorities have reportedly investigated whether Alice Huang, who has no family relationship to the Nvidia CEO, passed information to Beijing. The allegations have not established that Jensen Huang knowingly disclosed confidential information or violated export rules.

On Sunday, Nvidia's board announced an independent investigation into whether its chief executive was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation. The board said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets were compromised, but would examine the available information.

Earlier today, the Financial Times reported Nvidia has hired former U.S. prosecutor Joon H Kim to conduct the internal investigation.

The company is also facing legal pressure. Shareholders filed a proposed class-action lawsuit Monday alleging that Nvidia and Huang failed to disclose risks associated with the reported relationship.

The scandal has landed during a broader reassessment of the AI boom. Investors have questioned how long massive spending on data centers can continue, and Nvidia's competitors are offering customers more alternatives to its expensive chips.

Google introduced new AI chip configurations Monday. The Information reported that OpenAI was considering buying Google's tensor processing units, or TPUs, which are processors built specifically to run AI workloads. Neither company has confirmed a purchase but yesterday Google announced a new line of TPUs available for select customers, directly competing against Nvidia’s chips.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly earnings on November 17, one day before the congressional briefing requested by House Republicans. Mast's deadline for Huang to respond comes first, on November 11.

#5030. DogeOS ‘very, very close’ to launching DeFi features, app layer for Dogecoin: CEO

Jordan Jefferson says development and audits are complete as the team coordinates builders, wallets and exchanges ahead of launch.

DogeOS is nearing a mainnet launch that will let developers build apps and DeFi features for Dogecoin, founder and CEO Jordan Jefferson told The Block.

"Development is complete on version one; audits are complete," Jefferson said on a Wednesday episode of The Starting Block podcast. "Our main focus right now across the board is getting DogeOS to mainnet, which is very, very close."

The project is building an EVM-compatible app layer for Dogecoin that would give DOGE holders more ways to use the asset aside from just payments and tipping.

"We all love Dogecoin, but there wasn't much to do other than buy it, hold it, send it to your friends and tip people, which is awesome," Jefferson said. "But what if you could do more fun stuff?"

Jefferson said more than 100 builders have been working on projects and the team is working on supporting those developers and working to integrate with wallets, exchanges and other protocols.

This follows last month's launch of Dogecoin Labs, which brought together developers and products from DogeOS, the Dogecoin Foundation and House of Doge.

"Dogecoin is just too big to count on [donations] forever," he said.

#5028. Trump Media Posts $410 Million Profit. Bitcoin Gains Did the Heavy Lifting

Trump Media Posts $410 Million Profit. Bitcoin Gains Did the Heavy Lifting

Trump Media & Technology Group reported a $409.7 million third-quarter profit on Monday, a sharp turnaround for the company behind President Donald Trump's Truth Social.

However, most of the money that Trump Media & Technology made didn't come from media or technology. It came from the rising value of its crypto holdings and related investments.

The company generated just $2.97 million in revenue during the three months ended September 30, according to its SEC filing. A year earlier, it had reported a $54.8 million loss.

Trump Media recorded $496.1 million in realized and unrealized gains on digital assets, pledged crypto and equity securities, according to its earnings release. Much of that was an accounting gain—assets rose in value even when the company hadn't sold them for cash.

Digital assets and pledged coins accounted for $378.1 million of those gains, with securities adding another $118 million. Derivative losses and other expenses offset part of the windfall.

Bitcoin supplied a major boost. Chief Financial Officer Phillip Juhan said on the earnings call that it rose about 42% during the quarter, from roughly $59,000 at the end of June to $84,000 at the end of September.

Trump Media held approximately 15,649 Bitcoin at quarter-end, including around 11,385 coins pledged to a program designed to generate additional returns. Some of those assets are subject to arrangements allowing the counterparty to reuse the coins, according to the filing.

The company expanded into crypto in July 2025, purchasing about $2 billion in Bitcoin and related securities. That decision has tied its earnings closely to changes in digital-asset prices.

The downside was obvious just three months ago. Back in August Trump Media lost $238 million in the second quarter as declining crypto prices battered its holdings. This quarter, the accounting effect went in the opposite direction.

Truth Social's underlying business improved, though from a small base. Revenue increased 205% from about $973,000 a year earlier, supported by subscriptions for Truth+ streaming and Truth API, a paid service that gives customers faster access to posts on the platform that critics have likened to insider trading.

The company also recorded growth in advertising sales and fees from its Truth.Fi funds. Even so, Trump Media reported a $12.3 million adjusted EBITDA loss, a measure that excludes investment gains and certain other items, compared with a $29.1 million loss a year earlier.

The latest quarterly profit has not repaired the longer-term picture for shareholders. DJT shares traded around $4.20 Tuesday, up about 3.3% on the day but down 94.7% from their $79.38 registered back in 2024. The stock has lost nearly 70% this year.

Trump Media still posted a $234.2 million net loss for the first nine months of 2026. It is also pursuing a $6 billion merger with fusion-energy developer TAE Technologies, which shareholders are scheduled to vote on December 14.

Before that vote, holders of the company's $1 billion in convertible notes can demand cash repayment on November 30, according to its quarterly filing.

#5026. Global banks commit $270M to stablecoin venture targeting 2027 launch

Project Faro, backed by Goldman Sachs, Citigroup and BBVA, has secured $270 million in funding to launch a US dollar stablecoin in the first half of 2027.

A consortium of 21 financial institutions has committed about $270 million in funding to a stablecoin initiative known as Project Faro. The project, backed by big banks, aims to act as a stablecoin issuer and launch a US dollar stablecoin in the first half of 2027.

According to Bloomberg, the funding is intended to cover five years of operations for the new company, which plans to issue stablecoins pegged to the currencies of all G7 countries, with a euro-backed coin expected to follow the dollar launch.

The group first announced the initiative in September. While confirmed details are thin, people familiar with the matter said the company is expected to be incorporated in the US, Bloomberg reported.

The group includes banks such as Citigroup, Goldman Sachs, Banco Santander, Deutsche Bank and BBVA. Similar initiatives are also underway, including Stripe-led Open USD, which counts Visa among its founding partners, and Qivalis, a European banking consortium seeking to challenge the dominance of US dollar-pegged stablecoins.

BBVA is one of several Faro members also involved in competing stablecoin initiatives, including Open USD and Qivalis. Dutch lender Rabobank is also a Faro member and joined Qivalis in May, when the euro consortium added 25 banks to reach 37 members. BNP Paribas was among the 10 banks that first explored what became Faro in October 2025 and later joined Qivalis.

Qivalis has since made progress on the regulatory front, receiving authorization from the Dutch central bank as an electronic money institution in October 2026. The consortium is preparing to launch a fully regulated, euro-denominated stablecoin, with plans to make it available for payments, cross-border settlements and tokenized asset transactions.

The overlap highlights how traditional financial institutions are positioning themselves across multiple initiatives rather than committing exclusively to a single stablecoin issuance project.

By participating in competing projects, banks can explore different approaches to issuing and distributing digital currencies while retaining a stake in the infrastructure that could underpin future payment and settlement systems.

Related: BofA, Citi, Goldman Sachs among 21 institutions planning stablecoin launch

Despite the growing institutional interest, Faro is entering a market dominated by two crypto-native issuers.

Tether’s USDT and Circle’s USDC account for approximately 85% of the roughly $290 billion stablecoin market as of Nov. 10, according to DefiLlama. USDT had a market capitalization of approximately $176.3 billion, while USDC accounted for around $70.4 billion.

Distribution could, however, offer the bank-backed venture a competitive advantage. Faro’s participating institutions already have established banking customers, payment networks and relationships with businesses and financial institutions that could help distribute a new stablecoin.

Rather than building an entirely new customer base, the consortium could leverage those existing relationships to introduce its stablecoin to new audiences, including retail users. Open USD, developed by the Open Standard consortium, is pursuing a similar approach by bringing together payment providers, banks, financial technology companies and merchants around a shared stablecoin infrastructure.

The challenge for both will be translating their existing financial infrastructure and customer relationships into a competitive onchain payment network.

#5025. I Just Spent a Week in Iceland—Here's the Cold-Weather Capsule Wardrobe I'd Pack Again

The versatile staples and cozy essentials that made it into my suitcase.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I recently got back from a bucket-list trip: seven adventurous days in Iceland, chasing waterfalls, spotting rainbows, hiking remote canyons, and exploring the buzzy streets of Reykjavík. And while I may have perfected my travel capsule wardrobe, I’ll admit packing for Iceland was unlike any other destination.

I visited in late October, when temperatures hovered between 25 and 40 degrees Fahrenheit, though the wind often made it feel even colder. Iceland's weather can change dramatically throughout the day, so packing warm base layers and waterproof outerwear is essential. Between hiking to waterfalls, exploring the city's shops and restaurants, and long days in the car stopping at all the cool sights along the way, I needed clothes that could fend off wind, rain, sleet, and snow while regulating my body temp during everything from cool to freezing temps. But that didn’t mean sacrificing the gorpy-meets-girly outfits I wanted to wear (I am a style writer, after all).

Our first full day was a road trip from Reykjavík to Vík, which featured a beautiful string of waterfalls (or fosses, as they're called in Icelandic). It was a magical day, but also an incredibly wet one—so I was glad I'd packed plenty of waterproof gear. I walked behind the cascading water at Seljalandsfoss, took in the sheer scale and misty air of Skógafoss (which you might recognize from Game of Thrones), and fell particularly hard for Kvernufoss, a quieter waterfall tucked into a canyon that ended up being my personal favorite.

I ended up checking a large suitcase to make room for all my options—which is something I seldom do. (Not my smartest move, considering Icelandair charges a pretty penny for checked luggage!) In hindsight, I could have gotten away with much less if I'd been more strategic. So, I urge you to learn from my overpacking.

Ahead, I'm sharing the cold-weather capsule wardrobe I'd pack again for Iceland, including the waterproof essentials, versatile layers, and comfortable footwear that earned their place in my suitcase.

Weather can shift from bright and sunny to rainy and windy multiple times per day—and then you have the waterfalls to contend with, which will certainly have you soaked. So, a lightweight waterproof jacket is essential. Because I am extra, I packed three, but one or two is probably the magic number. The three coats below are the ones I actually wore: the water beaded right off all of these coats, and they were also super cute paired with my waterproof pants.

To go with your waterproof jacket, you’ll want at least one solid pair of waterproof, lightly insulated pants. You’ll have a base layer on underneath, so the pants don’t need to be too warm, but I think a plain shell pant might leave you feeling a bit chilly in the colder, rainier seasons. I brought three pairs of pants with me, but the two pairs below are the ones I would wholeheartedly pack again.

Halfdays Genevieve Gore-Tex 2L Insulated Pant

Initially, I thought a snowboot would be my most-worn shoe, but the conditions this time of year in southern Iceland made a waterproof hiking shoe more appropriate. If you visit in true winter or head further north, a snowboot might be your best bet. I recommend looking at the typical weather patterns for the region and time of year you're visiting, and keeping an eye on the forecast in the weeks and days leading up to your departure.

My trusted Merrell Moab 3 Mid waterproof boot ended up being perfect for our daily explorations, whether that involved misty waterfalls or moderate hiking. I also packed the Moonboot Icon Low as a snow boot option, and while they were undeniably stylish (and super warm), they weren’t the most useful for my particular visit. (They will certainly be my warm winter boot of choice this year, though!)

Warm wool socks are essential to wear under your hiking boots, not only for keeping your feet warm but also to help wick away moisture when things get sweaty. I wouldn’t skimp on socks—I brought six pairs (one for each active day of the trip).

Farm to Feet Damascus Merino Wool Crew Socks

When it comes to layering for winter or any cold-weather destination, base layers are key. These thermal tops and leggings help manage moisture to keep you warm and dry. I brought three sets, switching them out every two days, but if you’re keen to save more space, you could make do with two sets.

Iceland is not the place to rely on one chunky sweater to keep you warm. I found that having a few midweight wool sweaters and layers gave me much more flexibility, especially when temperatures and wind conditions changed throughout the day. Merino wool is particularly useful because it's warm and moisture-wicking without feeling overly bulky, making it easier to layer under a jacket.

I also brought a packable down jacket and a fleece to wear underneath my waterproof shell on especially cold days (or as an outer layer on warmer, drier days). Think of these as the middle layers in your outfit: You want enough warmth to stay comfortable, but enough flexibility to add or remove pieces as the weather changes.

I'd prioritize a warm, weather-resistant parka or winter coat for Iceland. If you opt for a fully waterproof parka (like the Finissterre Syke Waterproof Parka I brought with me), this can be your primary everyday coat. I personally preferred a lightweight shell jacket over my mid-layer for my daily adventures, though. But I liked having a longer jacket that provided extra coverage and warmth when I wasn't actively hiking—like while standing outside scanning the sky for Northern Lights or wandering downtown Reykjavik at night.

Look for something with a hood, a water-resistant or waterproof exterior, and enough room to accommodate a sweater or fleece underneath. Bonus points for something stylish enough to wear around the city without feeling like you're headed on an expedition.

Columbia Suttle Mountain Long Insulated Jacket

A warm beanie, insulated gloves, and a neck warmer or scarf might not feel as important as your sweaters and jackets, but you'll be grateful to have them when you're standing outside in the wind. I was particularly glad to have waterproof gloves and a warm hat on waterfall days and during our boat tour on the ice lagoon, when the combination of cold temperatures and misty spray made everything feel even chillier. I packed a few different options to go with my different outfits, but you could stick with one solid hat, scarf, or balaclava, and a pair of gloves to save space (though I was glad to have my chic leather gloves in Reykjavik!).

If you have one of the country's famous geothermal pools, hot springs, or lagoons on your itinerary, make room for a swimsuit and flip-flops. I visited Sky Lagoon, and though you can rent a swimsuit at most of the popular spots, having my own swimsuit along with a pair of easy slip-on shoes made the whole experience much more comfortable. These also take up practically no room in your suitcase, so this is one category where I wouldn't bother trying to cut back.

While most of my Iceland wardrobe revolved around technical layers and waterproof gear, I was glad to have one pair of regular pants for exploring Reykjavík. The city has a much more laid-back, everyday feel than some of the more rugged stops on the itinerary, and I didn't want to wear hiking pants to dinner or while popping into shops and cafés. A pair of jeans or casual travel pants is an easy way to feel a little more like yourself when you're off the trails. Just make sure they're comfortable enough for plenty of walking.

#5023. SEC enters fight over who regulates perpetual derivatives on stocks in the US

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The SEC filed an amicus brief, a court document in support of the CFTC after it was sued last week by the Capital Markets Alliance seeking to overturn approval of those products.

The Securities and Exchange Commission is stepping into the brewing fight over whether its sister agency should have approved perpetual derivatives on individual stocks in the U.S.

This week, the SEC filed an amicus brief, a court document in support of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission after it was sued last week by the Capital Markets Alliance seeking to overturn approval of those products. The CMA is an industry association with members including Cboe and Citadel Securities.

The SEC pushed back against the CMA's assertion that the CFTC has "gone rogue." The CFTC's new rule changes are also subject to the SEC's review and the SEC said it collaborates often with the CFTC, which is vital when it comes to overseeing equity perpetuals, the agency said in the brief.

"The SEC’s amicus brief addresses why that depiction—of a CFTC gone rogue, and an SEC gone missing—is mistaken," the agency said.

In its lawsuit, CMA said that the CFTC violated the Administrative Procedure Act — a law that governs how federal agencies propose rules — because the perpetuals derivatives should be under the SEC's jurisdiction. The CMA also argued that the CFTC did not explain its decision, and asked the court to "vacate the approvals."

Since then, a group of crypto firms including Coinbase, Kraken parent Payward, Kalshi, DRW Holdings, Paradigm and Jump Trading have filed a joint amicus brief against CMA.  Three of those firms — Coinbase, Kalshi and Payward — have submitted applications with the CFTC to list equity perps tied to U.S. stocks, and the applications were deemed approved on Nov. 2.

Others have also filed amicus briefs, including economic advocacy organization Better Markets and former SEC Commissioner Allison Herren Lee, who said that the new perpetuals should be regulated by the SEC.

"Although labeled by the CFTC and these trading platforms as ‘perpetual futures,’ these products are in fact securities swaps that are supposed to be regulated by the SEC with strong investor protections," Better Markets said in a statement on Tuesday. "That’s because these types of financial products contributed to the 2008 crash and pose an unreasonably high risk of causing total losses to retail investors."

#5022. Bitcoin Miner HIVE Eyes $45 Million in Annual Revenue From AI Deal

Bitcoin Miner HIVE Eyes $45 Million in Annual Revenue From AI Deal

Bitcoin miner HIVE Digital Technologies is eyeing $45 million in annual revenue from an AI data center in Sweden, but it still hasn't secured a final lease.

In its latest earnings update, HIVE reiterated that its 32-megawatt Big Boden facility could generate the sum through a long-term colocation arrangement. That revenue depends on a definitive agreement, financing, facility upgrades, and the project entering service.

The company first disclosed the proposal in June. It involves an unnamed, investment-grade Swedish technology company and contemplates a lease lasting up to 10 years. During Tuesday's earnings call, CEO Aydin Kilic said the parties were negotiating deposits, termination rights, and other terms that could affect financing costs. A lender's consultant had visited the site the previous week, he said.

“We do not want to rush into a deal just for the sake of reporting new top-line revenue,” Kilic said, according to the earnings call transcript.

HIVE expects to seek financing for the Boden project after securing a signed lease, potentially through corporate bonds. The company has not announced a completion date for negotiations.

The existing data center would also need a substantial makeover. HIVE plans to retrofit it for up to 10,000 Nvidia GB300 GPUs, using liquid cooling to handle high-density computing racks.

Although the facility has a 32-megawatt utility connection, the proposed customer would receive approximately 25 megawatts of usable computing capacity, with the remaining power supporting data center operations.

“This site has evolved from a GPU compute facility supporting Ethereum,” Kilic said when announcing the letter of intent, describing its planned conversion into a more powerful AI installation.

The expansion follows HIVE's broader effort to move beyond Bitcoin mining, a shift that has already produced several large GPU-cloud contracts.

HIVE signed a major three-year AI computing agreement with Bell Canada and Cohere in June, covering 2,304 Nvidia GPUs as it expanded beyond crypto mining.

HIVE now reports $185 million in contracted GPU-cloud annualized run-rate revenue, although only $40 million is live. The remainder represents contracted capacity that has not yet entered service.

The company is targeting approximately $700 million in total high-performance computing annualized revenue by the end of 2028, though that figure includes projects requiring additional financing, construction, and customer commitments.

For Boden, HIVE's next milestone is securing a definitive lease before proceeding with project financing.

#5020. Strategy, Metaplanet lose MSCI index membership in November reshuffle

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The index changes take effect Nov. 30, following months of debate over the eligibility of crypto treasury companies.

MSCI is moving to delist bitcoin treasury companies Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) from its USA Index and Metaplanet (3350.T) from its Japan Small Cap Index at the close of Nov. 30, according to filings published Wednesday.

The removals were mostly expected, especially after MSCI adopted new eligibility rules for so-called non-operating companies last month, which put several crypto treasury firms on the proverbial chopping block. Ethereum treasury company Sharplink was not listed among the deletions in the November small-cap review.

The new rules look at companies' operating assets and five financial measures, replacing temporary restrictions on firms that held at least half their assets in crypto. MSCI previously said that companies on its indexes would face removal if they failed this screening for their two most recent annual filing periods.

Strategy Executive Chairman Michael Saylor and Phong Le previously spoke out against the rule change, calling the proposed treatment of digital asset treasuries "discriminatory." In a September letter, Strategy argued that exclusion would have no meaningful impact on its business but would harm MSCI's reputation as a neutral index provider.

MSCI's review documents did not give company-specific explanations for the removals.

Strategy's common MSTR shares fell 2.6% in after-hours trading to $119.06. Metaplanet shares closed Wednesday down 2.53% at 231 yen in Tokyo, before MSCI announced the removals.

#5018. CFTC warns Kalshi of imminent charges over notification of contracts: Politico

The prediction markets operator says the issue involves procedural errors affecting a handful of contracts, many of which weren’t live, according to Politico.

Prediction markets operator Kalshi is being warned of imminent charges from the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission over whether the operator properly notified the agency about new products, Politico reported Tuesday.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is investigating notifications involving dozens of products, the report said, citing a person with direct knowledge of the probe. The agency’s private notification to Kalshi suggests that charges could follow.

The investigation has been underway for months, the person said.

Speaking to Politico, Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana described the matter as a procedural error involving submissions for a handful of contracts, many of which never went live.

“We are already addressing the issue,” she added.

The potential enforcement action comes as the CFTC fights to establish its exclusive authority over prediction markets, including by challenging state efforts to regulate Kalshi.

Separately, Kalshi is backing the regulator in a separate legal battle over its authority. On Monday, the company joined Coinbase and four other firms in seeking permission to file a brief supporting the CFTC’s treatment of perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks.

Related: Coinbase, Kalshi join lawsuit backing CFTC over US stock perp futures

#5016. LNbits rushes emergency patch after reported Bitcoin wallet thefts

The project warned that installations running older versions were vulnerable and urged operators to replace potentially exposed credentials even after upgrading.

Bitcoin Lightning wallet platform LNbits has urged users to update immediately to its latest version after reporting a wave of attacks targeting LNbits instances that resulted in stolen funds.

The project published version 1.7.0 on Tuesday, describing it as a critical release addressing vulnerabilities and strengthening authentication, extension management and payment handling. LNbits warned that updating alone would not protect installations whose credentials had already been compromised.

“It’s been a very challenging few days, working relentlessly to get the v1.7 release out after our users and we suffered losses,” LNbits creator Ben Arc said on X.

LNbits provides software for running Bitcoin Lightning wallets and payment applications, including merchant checkouts, tipping services and paywalls. Developer Ben Arc said in May that about 200,000 people run the software themselves.

LNbits first warned operators on Sunday to disable its Paywall extension while it investigated a potential vulnerability. Just over an hour later, the project urged users to shut down their LNbits servers immediately, citing a wave of attacks. It also took its own hosted installations offline.

The company has not yet detailed the exploit and how many were impacted by it. In an announcement accompanying the emergency patch, LNbits said the vulnerability affects all versions before 1.7.0 and puts funds at risk of theft.

Related: BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

It also warned that credentials exposed before upgrading could still give attackers access afterward, urging operators to reset wallet keys, replace funding-source credentials and treat secrets stored in LNbits settings as compromised and recommended moving to a fresh server if a compromise is suspected.

Cointelegraph reached out to Ben Arc and LNbits for comment.

One user, Kevin Ravensberg, said attackers exploited his LNbits instance, withdrawing about 2.3 million satoshis, or 0.023 Bitcoin, through newly created accounts before he could shut down the server.

The company said a separate report detailing the exploit would follow.

#5015. Prisoner Jailed for 8 Years Over $2.7M Crypto Theft Plot Run From His Cell

Prisoner Jailed for 8 Years Over $2.7M Crypto Theft Plot Run From His Cell

A man who directed an attempted £2 million ($2.7 million) cryptocurrency theft from his prison cell has been jailed for eight years by Scotland's High Court of Justiciary.

Robert Barr, 29, pleaded guilty along with Sean Favier, 26, and Iain Doyle, 37, to being involved in serious organised crime as part of the attempted theft, according to a sentencing statement published by Scotland's Judicial Office on Tuesday. Lord Cubie sentenced Favier to four years and Doyle to five years at the High Court in Glasgow.

"This was a crime which married 21st century computer sophistication with old fashioned housebreaking in an audacious attempt to steal cryptocurrency worth up to £2 million pounds," Lord Cubie said.

The plan, according to the court's account, was for Favier and Doyle to get into the victims' home in Midlothian, near Edinburgh, and make the man go onto his computer and download AnyDesk, a remote-access program. That would let Barr, who was serving a 21-month sentence at HMP Edinburgh for fraud, take control of the computer from prison and transfer the crypto to wallets he controlled.

The two men went to the house at around 11:30 p.m. on April 17, 2024, but the couple saw them on their doorbell camera and refused to let them in. One of the men looked into the lens and asked whether there was a camera in front of him before the pair left, the BBC reported.

The attempt followed months of preparation, including identifying the victim as a crypto holder, gathering images of the house and reconnaissance, all of which investigators pieced together from Barr's phone after it was seized in June 2024, the judge said. Messages on the phone "plainly contemplated threats and/or violence," he added.

The victim was a co-founder of a crypto startup who did not advertise his holdings, but followed mostly crypto-related accounts on social media, according to the BBC. Barr's associates had already burgled the house in January 2024 and taken a crypto wallet, but could not access the funds without its PIN, the court heard.

Prosecutors described Barr as a "sophisticated cybercriminal" able to "orchestrate a level of criminality rarely seen within mainstream investigations." He had a previous conviction for a crypto fraud, and organised the new offence while in prison "using plainly illicit means of being in contact or online," Lord Cubie said, calling those materially aggravating factors.

The judge said that the plot was only frustrated because the victims "had the sense not to open their front door" to unknown men with their faces covered. Lord Cubie will consider a serious crime prevention order against Barr on November 17. Barr is also in custody awaiting potential extradition, the judge noted.

Had the men got inside, the plot would likely have escalated into a so-called wrench attack, in which criminals use force or the threat of it to make crypto holders hand over their funds. Such attacks are on pace for a record year, with more than $30 million stolen in the first half of 2026, according to a Chainalysis report.

The UK has seen several recent cases. Three teenagers were jailed earlier this year after stealing $4.3 million in crypto at knifepoint, and three men were sentenced last month over a failed home invasion aimed at stealing crypto. In September, Crimestoppers offered a reward of up to £10,000 for information on an attack on a UK couple in which, police believe, a fourth man relayed instructions to the three attackers over a video call.

#5013. 9 Best Winter Boots for Women, Tested and Reviewed by Fashion Editors

After years of testing, our top pick is from Sorel.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As a style writer, I live in comfortable sneakers and heels for most of the year. But come winter, my favorite shoes can’t handle New York City’s slick, slushy streets. That’s when I swap in a tried-and-true waterproof winter boot. For the past two years, WH editors have been testing tons of cold-weather shoes and have found the best options that keep our feet warm without skimping on style.

During evaluations, we put a range of comfortable boots to the test, including chic leather options for warmer climates, warm hiking boots, and insulated styles for the chilliest locales. We wore them during long, snowy days on Finland’s slopes and wet morning commutes to our New York City office. The result? A curated guide to the absolute best options for different preferences and weather conditions.

What’s New: - At the close of last winter, we added The North Face Thermoball Lace Up Waterproof Boots to our guide. Dorenna Newton, executive producer for Men’s Health and Women’s Health, tested this boot in St. Moritz while covering Team USA’s bobsled training ahead of the Olympics. - In our November 2026 update, I added the Columbia Snowtrot Mid Boot and the Moon Boot Icon Low Pearly Plus after testing both pairs in Iceland. Both scored major style points, while the Snowtrot excelled in wet conditions and the Moon Boot won me over with its extreme warmth and comfort when temps plummeted.- The Columbia Minx Shorty IV Boot continues to prove its top-notch water resistance and warmth. This boot won me over in Finland last year, and I wore it nonstop during winter 2026 in NYC while navigating slushy streets and freezing temps.- We’re currently testing a handful of more winter boots as temperatures start to drop and will update our guide with the best new styles we find.

We were first drawn to the Sorel Caribou boot because its laces and colorways look more stylish than most other winter boots. But after testing it for over two years, we found it’s also warm enough to handle super-cold temps and repels water like a pro.

Starting with style, the quilted padding, metal hardware, and lace-up front closure add a nice design touch that separates this from other serious winter boots. It pairs well with jeans, leggings, or even a cool maxi skirt or dress. We love the orange color, but if that shade is a little too much for you, the brand also makes beige and black styles that pair well with any outfit.

These boots also have lugged soles for excellent traction and are insulated and waterproof to keep you warm and dry in serious weather conditions. The upper is made from high-quality leather with a seam-sealed construction to keep moisture out. Plus, they have a rubber mudguard at the toes for extra warmth and protection, and a removable synthetic liner so you can customize your level of insulation.

“The fit is secure, so nothing is getting through these babies,” says Saladino, who wears this for snowy weekdays in New York City and ski weekends. “They’re perfect for après-skiing, shoveling the driveway, or anytime you need complete and total protection from the elements,” she says.

Her only complaint? They are a little on the heavier side, weighing in at two pounds and one ounce.

This cozy boot is the perfect pull-on style for casual snowy days and quick outings, Saladino says. Case in point: it's ideal when you need to take the dog out or you’re jetting out the door for school drop-off. There’s also extra room inside the boot, which Saladino loves when she needs to slip it on and go.

Despite the short style, the weatherproof suede boot is plenty warm. The faux-fur lining adds a cozy layer of insulation. We also like how light it is, even though it has a thick rubber outsole that’s super grippy on slippery surfaces.

Plus, it has a clean, simple design and cozy-chic look that you’ll actually want to wear all winter long. We love the contrast between the suede upper and the lighter-colored outsole, and think this pair looks adorable styled with a pair of wool socks and leggings.

Our only word of warning is that because the shaft height on this boot is on the shorter side, it may not be ideal for extra snowy days. If you need a boot to handle more intense winter weather, we recommend opting for The North Face Shellista IV Mid WP.

On an October trip to Iceland, I opted for this waterproof pair, since fall there is super unpredictable and rainy. While I opted for hiking boots most of the time, the Columbia Snowtrot Boot proved its waterproof capabilities on a particularly wet day of the trip, not letting a drop of water inside the shoe.

Considering how warm it kept my feet, I know I’ll be reaching for these all winter in New York. I love the puffy silhouette with its quilted upper and the monochrome finish in the all black colorway (which looks way more streamlined than similar pairs I’ve seen with white or gum soles). It’s lightweight, easy to pack, and has a sleek look that styles well with leggings and a puffer jacket or sweater. I especially like that they don't have the bulky feel I’ve come to expect from a winter boot.

Where this boot really stands out is its weather protection. It’s made with multiple layers of breathable synthetic materials and reinforced seams that help keep rain and moisture out. It also has 200 grams of insulation and a soft fleece interior to keep feet warm and cozy.

I also appreciate the side zipper, which makes them easy to slip on and off (especially handy when putting on your boots while bending over in bulky pants and jackets). The rubber outsole also helps provide tons of traction on both wet and dry surfaces, with a grippy tread pattern that comes in handy while navigating rainy sidewalks, muddy paths, or icy ground.

While it costs a fraction of the price of other winter boots, Dream Pairs makes a solid winter shoe that keeps cold and wet weather out. In addition to a water-resistant faux leather, the shoe has a plush faux-fur interior that’s super insulating. It also has a 1.37-inch rubber anti-slip sole for better traction in slippery conditions. The sole has 2mm of plush memory foam for added comfort and shock absorption. We also appreciate the pull tab on the back of the boot, which makes them easier to slip on and off.

Like our top overall pick, we like that this winter boot comes in a variety of colors: beige, nude, light gray, chestnut, and black. The cozy faux-fur lining, monochromatic laces, side panel stitching, and sturdy sole make it super stylish and practical. It easily slips under snow and ski pants, and looks just as cute styled with a pair of leggings and fuzzy socks.

On the downside, our testers said they noticed signs of wear and tear sooner than other pairs, particularly ripping of the faux-fur lining.

When I started crafting my packing list for a recent trip to Northern Finland, a cute but snow-proof winter boot was at the top of my list. After lots of research, I landed on the Columbia Minx Shorty IV Boot—a perfect blend of style, traction, water resistance, and warmth.

The moment I slipped this boot on, I loved the cozy, faux-fur insulation that kept my feet warm even on frigid mornings and evenings. And the waterproof nylon outer meant I didn’t have to worry about slushy streets or fresh snow seeping through. The traction is also super impressive, and I felt comfortable walking over icy sidewalks, a frozen lake, and snowy trails.

The shaft height hits about two inches above the ankle, offering just the right amount of coverage for typical city snow or moderate winter adventures. While deeper snow might call for a taller boot, this pair provides the ideal balance between protection and mobility.

The cherry on top? It’s genuinely so stylish. The sleek design, color selection, and faux-fur collar mean you don’t have to sacrifice fashion for function. I’ve paired them with everything from fleece-lined leggings and Fair Isle sweaters, snow pants and a winter coat, and even jeans—and they always look great. It was my go-to boot for the whole trip, and I know I’ll get just as much use out of it this winter in New York.

If your feet are perpetually cold once winter hits, meet your new favorite snow boots. The boot features an extremely thick layer of insulation in the form of a cushioned foam lining that molds to your feet over time. Wearing this boot genuinely feels like stepping into a giant marshmallow. I especially like that the platforms lift your feet off the cold, wet ground, which makes a huge difference in keeping them toasty. Plus, the shearling details add a cozy touch.

I also love Moon Boot’s iconic, space-age silhouette with its oversized shape. The Italian brand has built a reputation for seriously chic après-ski style, but don't let the fashion-forward appearance fool you. The rubber tread outsole provides plenty of grip on snowy surfaces, while the surprisingly lightweight feel makes them comfortable for everyday winter outings, whether you're heading to the lodge or walking around town after a fresh snowfall.

Fun fact: These boots are ambidextrous, meaning there's no designated left or right boot, and they mold to your feet as you wear them (though I will say they feel really good straight out of the box).

After testing this boot in Iceland, I can say that having a genuinely warm, insulated pair of boots makes a big difference when you're outside in the cold for extended periods. This pair is hands-down my favorite option for especially frigid locations when warmth is your biggest priority, particularly if you're looking for something that doesn't sacrifice style.

Just note that the upper is water-repellent rather than fully waterproof, so I'd save them for snowy conditions rather than slushy streets or rainy days.

This boot scores major style points, whether you’re on the mountain or navigating slushy city streets. We love the nude tone (with a subtle sheen), horizontal stitching, and puffy silhouette. Toggle laces keep the look neat and polished, while branded pull loops on both sides add functional visual interest.

Dorenna Newton, executive producer for Men’s Health and Women’s Health, recently wore this boot in St. Moritz while covering Team USA’s bobsled training ahead of the Olympics. She loved that they looked “super chic” for après adventures in the village—earning style points along with practicality.

“I’m constantly on the move, so packing light is non-negotiable,” Newton says. “That means every item has to earn its place—including this brand-new pair of The North Face snow boots I decided to test in real time.” She says this boot delivered from the moment she slipped it on, feeling instantly comfortable while providing serious warmth on frigid mountainsides.

The shaft hits right above the ankle and delivers impressive warmth thanks to recycled polyester insulation and a cozy fleece lining. A drawcord cinches the boot at the ankle for added protection from cold air, snow, and rain. Paired with a durable, waterproof ripstop upper made from recycled water bottles, the boot fares well under any condition.

Like the The North Face Shellista IV, this boot has exceptional traction thanks to a grippy, rubber outsole, which Newton says kept her steady on icy ground. However, like our other pick from The North Face, this boot has a snug, rigid fit—so consider sizing up if you’re between sizes or have a wider foot.

We’re big fans of L.L. Bean’s iconic Duck Boot, and this fleece version is perfect for chilly weather. “I love the rugged weekend styling of this boot,” says Saladino. “It’s super cozy on the inside, and the fleece detailing and contrasting laces on the outside are cool styling touches.”

It’s also well-equipped for slushy snow, thanks to a waterproof leather upper to keep moisture out. Plus, it has a rubber outsole with a grooved tread pattern that helps you stay upright and balanced on slick sidewalks. Bonus: The boot is made with 200 grams of PrimaLoft insulation, an ultra-warm, quick-drying material to keep feet dry and toasty.

The brand doesn’t have half sizes, but we found it wise to size down if you’re between sizes. “I am a size 9, and it fits great even with a thick sock,” says Saladino. Overall, we were impressed by both style and function—particularly the traction, which makes it a bit more equipped for heavy winter conditions than other ankle-length styles.

The Ugg Classic Mini Platform is a well-loved classic, and freelance stylist Alexandra Juric says they're constantly in rotation during the fall and winter. It’s stylish, comfortable, and warm. It has that classic Ugg boot shape but with a trendy platform that gives total cool-girl vibes. But you'd better hurry up and *add to cart* if you want this style ahead of the holidays—it’s known to sell out quickly.

The inside of the boot is lined with the brand’s signature wool blend, which adds a fluffy layer of insulation and comfort. It also has a heavily cushioned EVA foam midsole that feels super plush under the foot. The suede outer adds to the cozy look and feel, though it’s less water-resistant than other leathers. We recommend snagging the shoe protector spray if you plan to wear them in wet weather. We also love that the added two-inch platform to the classic silhouette gives you height without sacrificing comfort or traction.

Associate health and fitness editor Addison Aloian says this boot is the perfect addition to her winter wardrobe. "I've worn them on wet, rainy, snowy streets and they've stayed relatively clean, even in a lighter color," she says. "They're insanely comfortable with the plush interior, warm in even the coldest temps, and totally cradle my tired feet—I feel like I could walk miles in them!" She loves that they are super stylish and go with just about any outfit, but she's partial to styling them with a long trouser or flared leggings.

The downside? They don’t come in half sizes and tend to run big, so keep that in mind when shopping.

The type of boot you want for everyday city commuting is different from what you'd need for winter hiking or snowshoeing in seriously cold weather, says Julie Hardy, senior buyer at Zappos.

While a boot’s temperature ratings provide a useful starting point for what to look for when shopping, there's no one-size-fits-all rule, says Hardy. For most outdoor activities like snowshoeing, hiking, or backpacking, our experts suggest looking for a baseline of about 200 grams of insulation. Liners can also boost the product's warmth. Seam-sealed and waterproof features are also important if you spend a good amount of time in the snow or rain. Look for terms like "Thinsulate" and "GORE-TEX," both of which are trademarked materials that provide superior insulation.

Keep in mind that taller boots also help keep more of your leg warm even without the presence of snow. Generally, mid-height boots (about 6.5 inches) strike the perfect balance of coverage and comfort; however, tall heights (12 inches or more) are better suited for deep snow. Ultimately, shaft height is about personal preference, but make sure your boots go above the hem of your pants to keep snow and cold air out.

As a fashion writer who’s tried countless pairs of shoes over the past ten years, I know a thing or two about what makes a solid winter boot. I went to school in chilly upstate New York for four years, have a lifetime of NYC winters under my belt, and regularly travel to places with extreme conditions (most recently, Iceland and Finnish Lapland—above the Arctic Circle).

We first wrote this article in November 2023 with our team’s all-time favorite winter boots. But since we’re always testing new pairs each winter and during cold-weather vacations, I’m constantly updating the list with the absolute best winter boots from brands like Sorel, Columbia, Moon Boot, The North Face, Merrell, Ugg, and L.L. Bean.

I also consulted a senior buyer for Zappos, which is one of the largest footwear retailers out there, on what to consider when shopping for winter boots: materials, insulation, and shaft height. WH fashion contributors Kristin Saladino, Rose Lauture, Lily Wohlner, and freelance stylist Alexandra Juric also weighed in on their favorite options. Collectively, we have over 30 years of experience testing shoes and boots.

#5012. Crypto ally Tom Emmer elected House GOP minority whip

The Minnesota lawmaker, who helped establish the Congressional Crypto Caucus, will stay on as Republican whip after Democrats won the midterms.

Pro-crypto US Representative Tom Emmer has been elected House Republican minority whip, securing the party’s second-highest leadership position as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January.

Emmer beat Texas Representative August Pfluger in a 103-76 secret ballot vote, according to Politico. He previously served as House Republican majority whip and will remain the party’s No. 2 leader, this time under incoming Minority Leader Jim Jordan.

As minority whip, Emmer will be responsible for rallying Republican votes, coordinating the party’s legislative strategy and helping maintain unity on key bills.

Emmer has been a leading Republican voice on digital assets. In March 2025, he and Representative Ritchie Torres announced the bipartisan Congressional Crypto Caucus, aimed at mobilizing support for digital asset legislation, including stablecoin and market structure bills.

He is also an original cosponsor of the CLARITY Act and introduced the Blockchain Regulatory Certainty Act, which seeks to clarify that blockchain developers and infrastructure providers who do not control customer funds are not money transmitters under federal law.

“Tom has been one of the strongest advocates for digital assets in Congress, and he has led the fight against a government-controlled CBDC and stood up for American innovators,” GOP Senator Cynthia Lummis said on X.

#5010. Winter’s Most Wanted: Tommy Hilfiger Holiday Picks for Every Occasion

From sharp knits to travel-ready extras, these are the pieces worth putting on your list.

The sweater that looked fine in October suddenly feels too thin, the jacket you reach for every day isn’t warm enough, and a weekend away calls for more than whatever you can squeeze into an overnight bag. Then there’s the rest of the season: office parties, cold walks across town, slow mornings at home, and the occasional trip out of town. Getting dressed for all of it takes a few reliable essentials.

Tommy Hilfiger has been building its take on American style since 1985, when the brand launched with a fresh spin on classic menswear. Its signature mix of preppy staples, sporty details, and relaxed ease has made it a year-round proposition, with pieces that move from everyday dressing to more seasonal needs. For winter, that means familiar wardrobe staples with a little extra warmth, practical layers for unpredictable weather, and accessories suited to life on the go. These six picks cover the season from the first cold snap to the last weekend getaway. Here’s what we’re putting on our fashion gift list.

A cable-knit sweater is a cold-weather staple, and Tommy Hilfiger’s take keeps the look classic. Made from combed cotton with a traditional cable pattern and ribbed trim, it’s an easy layer under a wool coat or a natural choice for a casual holiday gathering. Pair it with dark jeans or chinos for a straightforward winter look.

For trips, a jacket that barely takes up any room in your bag is always useful. Tommy Hilfiger’s lightweight puffer folds into its own interior zip pocket, making it easy to stash for a weekend away or keep on hand when the forecast is unpredictable. It has thermal insulation, a regular fit, and side pockets, so it’s a practical layer for travel and everyday errands alike.

A good winter evening often calls for staying in, and this sleep set makes a comfortable night at home feel a little more put together. This one pairs a simple T-shirt with plaid flannel pants, bringing a classic cold-weather pattern into the mix. It’s an easy gift for someone who appreciates a slow weekend morning as much as a cozy night in.

A warm beanie is one of winter’s most useful accessories, and this one gets an upgrade with a cotton-cashmere blend. The classic knit is an easy finishing touch for everything from a morning commute to a weekend walk. It’s also a practical gift that will see plenty of use once the temperature drops.

A card holder and keychain are the kind of everyday essentials that make an especially easy gift. Tommy Hilfiger’s leather set brings the two together in one package, with a polished finish that feels a step up from the usual stocking stuffer. It’s a useful pick for someone who likes to keep their daily carry simple.

A weekend away calls for a bag that can handle a change of clothes and a few extra essentials without looking like you’re heading to the gym. Tommy’s leather duffel brings a more polished feel to the overnight bag, making it a smart companion for a winter getaway or a quick trip home for the holidays.

#5009. Avalanche Won Over the NYSE. Now It Has to ‘Capture Value’ for AVAX

Avalanche Won Over the NYSE. Now It Has to ‘Capture Value’ for AVAX

The New York Stock Exchange this week selected Avalanche to provide infrastructure for a tokenized securities platform it's prepping. Experts say that it's a big deal for Avalanche's reputation, but warn that it may not translate into an uptick in value for the network's AVAX token.

On Monday, Ava Labs announced Avalanche would serve as the "core ledger technology" for a tokenized securities platform developed by NYSE parent company Intercontinental Exchange. The product will be designed to allow 24/7 trading, clearing, and settlement of tokenized securities, subject to regulatory approval.

Carlos Guzman, research analyst at market maker GSR, told Decrypt it’s “definitely a big win for the project.”

“Having NYSE choose its tech for settlement is a big institutional credibility boost, and it validates the benefits blockchain tech can provide to traditional financial plumbing with instant settlement and programmability,” he said.

Luther Maday, head of North America at crypto payments firm Mercuryo, told Decrypt that the decision came after a year of testing on Avalanche, which signals the network passed checks on "performance, reliability, and control."

"NYSE has chosen the technology for the clearing and settlement layer, which is a much deeper use of blockchain than simply issuing a tokenized asset on a network," Maday added.

Avalanche's AVAX token has risen 3.6% in the past day to $8.64, according to Decrypt data. But experts warn that providing infrastructure doesn't necessarily mean a long-term increase in token value.

"NYSE will be using Avalanche’s infrastructure for clearing and settlement, so the actual trading will still happen off-chain," Guzman explained.

This means NYSE activity will run on dedicated Avalanche infrastructure rather than the public C-Chain, where most of the network's activity happens.

"I would be careful about translating the news directly into expectations for activity on the public C-Chain," Maday said.

That said, Maday said to keep an eye on the details of how the AVAX token is used within the dedicated infrastructure. He also suggested that stablecoin liquidity, validator activity, and network fees could see a boost.

The NYSE has also teamed up with Blockchain.com to sell tokenized stocks and ETFs to crypto investors, while its parent company ICE is working with OKX on a 24/7 tokenized stock venue under an SEC exemption. Morgan Stanley, meanwhile, has started tokenizing securities of its own.

Not every company wants in, though. All seven of the so-called "Magnificent Seven" stocks—Apple, Meta, and the like—opted out of having their shares tokenized on the OKX-ICE venue, along with dozens of other companies.

Even if tokenization does take off, that doesn't guarantee profits for AVAX investors.

"The main question for Avalanche is whether it can do what other blockchains have struggled to do: capture value," Misha Putiatin, founder of collateral markets platform Symbiotic, told Decrypt. "Avalanche has always had an unusually outcome-focused team for this industry, so I suspect they have plans for that, but it remains to be seen."

Ava Labs did not immediately respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#5007. Across Protocol restores website after hackers hijack domain

The cross-chain bridge said a social engineering attack targeting domain registrar Namecheap allowed hackers to redirect users to a phishing site.

Cross-chain bridge Across Protocol said it has regained control of its website after a social engineering attack on Tuesday enabled attackers to gain control of its domain, replacing its website with a phishing site.

In a post on Wednesday, Across said the across.to domain is back under their control with help from Namecheap founder Richard Kirkendall, and that all systems are now working normally.

“All affected users will be made whole. We’ll share a full post-mortem in the coming days,” the team said on X.

The update follows Across’s Tuesday warning to users to avoid its app amid reports of drained wallets. The total losses and number of affected users have not yet been disclosed.

In an earlier update, Across said a social engineering attack on its domain registrar, Namecheap, gave an attacker control of its domain at approximately 18:45 UTC.

The team said it was alerted within 15 minutes and immediately worked to shut the website down. Security response group SEAL911 helped flag the domain as phishing with major wallets, while Cloudflare marked it as at risk and later removed its DNS records.

Some users who visited the domain before the malicious site was fully blocked may have been affected, the team said. Third-party integrations and API users were unaffected.

The incident affected one of the largest cross-chain bridges by trading activity. Across ranked third by 30-day transfer volume, behind Circle and LayerZero, according to DefiLlama, having processed about $5.9 billion over the period.

Related: Across Protocol warns users to avoid app amid reports of drained wallets
