#5006. Rose Lavelle Suits Up to Build Gotham FC’s Future

Committed to the reigning NWSL champions for three more seasons, the soccer star reflects on leadership, life lessons, and a monumental move to a shiny new stadium in New York City.

Rose Lavelle is breaking down her outfit of the day, though it’s not the ensemble you’d expect from a professional athlete’s social media feed.

“My hard hat, which is… something,” the Gotham FC midfielder says with a chuckle. She looks down to survey the rest of her mandatory construction-site-chic attire. “My gloves—gardener’s. My boots—too big. My vest—cool.”

It’s a Tuesday afternoon in September, and Rose is the first of her teammates to tour the unfinished Etihad Park in Queens, New York, the stadium that Gotham will call home in 2028. Earlier that day, Gotham announced that Rose had signed a contract extension through 2029. In other words, she'll be back when this concrete floor is an actual soccer pitch.

We weave past steel beams and through what will eventually become Gotham's tunnel to the pitch. From there, the grandstands rise around us, a preview of the 25,000-seat venue that will soon welcome Gotham's fans. Rose poses for photos, holding a jersey that says “2029.”

A few days later, Gotham’s president of soccer operations, Yael Averbuch West, tells me she smiled when she saw the images of Rose in her hard hat. The look may be a departure from her usual uniform, but Averbuch West sees something fitting about it. As Gotham prepares to make another postseason run in pursuit of defending their 2025 National Women’s Soccer League title, Rose has become central to the club's plans.

“She has been part of literally constructing this vision with us,” Averbuch West says. “She has been an instrumental part of the vision of the club and our aspirations and our ambition.”

The roughly $780 million Etihad Park is being developed by Major League Soccer's New York City FC, which will share the stadium with Gotham beginning in 2028. It's a significant upgrade from Gotham's current arrangement at Sports Illustrated Stadium in Harrison, New Jersey, where the team uses the visitors' locker room and covers up the New York Red Bulls’ logos with its own banners. At Etihad Park, Gotham will have a locker room of its own, merchandise space in the team store, and digital signage and video boards that can be fully branded for Gotham on match days—no covering up another team's logos required.

The move is another sign of just how much women's professional soccer has changed since Rose entered the NWSL nearly a decade ago. And in that time, Rose has changed, too. At 31, the once up-and-coming star has become a veteran helping set the standard for a club that has no plans to slow down.

The NWSL is not the same league it was when Rose was drafted first overall by the now-defunct Boston Breakers in 2017, playing home games at a 2,500-seat facility at Harvard University. When I sit down with Rose after our stadium tour, she tells me it’s difficult to compare the things that mattered to her as a rookie to signing a contract extension now. “I think of the amount of support staff we had, it was so few—how many people were dedicated towards ticketing and marketing and all of that,” she says. “I notice that stuff a lot more now than I probably did at the time, because you don't know what you don't know.”

Rose has come a long way since then. She won the 2019 World Cup with the U.S. Women’s National Team, earned Olympic gold in Paris in 2024, and navigated injuries and moves between NWSL clubs. She joined Gotham in 2024 and, after ankle surgery sidelined her for the start of her second season, returned to help the club win the 2025 NWSL championship, scoring the winning goal. In January, she was named U.S. Soccer's 2025 Female Player of the Year.

On the field, Rose’s influence isn't always captured in goals or assists. Gotham rookie Andrea Kitahata calls her the club’s “quiet momentum-builder and tone-setter” for how she initiates the team's defensive press and creates attacking opportunities that may not show up on a stat sheet.

Rose has learned plenty from USWNT veterans like Megan Rapinoe, Becky Sauerbrunn, Alex Morgan, Christen Press, and Tobin Heath, who showed her that there was no single way to command respect in a locker room. “Everybody led in their own way,” Rose says. “Everybody's leadership style might have looked different, but it wasn't any more or less impactful than the other.”

Now, the younger players in Gotham’s locker room are learning from Rose—especially since her 2025 ankle surgery, the first of her career, which forced her to find new ways to contribute off the field.

She initially thought surgery would be a straightforward fix. “I think I thought you get surgery to fix it, and when you're off the table, you’re fixed, and it's smooth sailing. And it was not smooth sailing at all,” she says. She pauses. “Yeah, it was really painful! It was really painful.”

The physical recovery was only part of the challenge. Rose says the mental toll of rehabbing—and the uncertainty that came with it—tested her in ways she hadn't anticipated. “It taught me I had a whole ’nother level of mental grind and fortitude that I could battle through,” she says. “I think you learn the most about yourself in the tough moments. I mean, it was hard—but I made it through, and I feel like I gained so much.”

Rose has never been the loudest voice in the locker room, but recent years have taught her that she doesn’t need to be.

Ahead of the 2025 season, Rose told Averbuch West that she would be more intentional about creating the change she wanted to see within the club. “When you're Rose Lavelle, you are a leader, whether you choose to be or not,” Averbuch West says. “It doesn't have to come in big speeches in front of the group. It’s not going to be that. But it's the little things she does behind the scenes, the little connections with players—pulling someone aside, talking on the field.”

Her teammates take cues from her, Averbuch West says. If Gotham is down a goal at halftime, they look to Rose to gauge how she's responding. If Rose is inspired, they’re inspired. And Rose has become increasingly intentional about the example she sets. “She's been very proactive during her time here to share really constructive feedback with us, and to also be part of creating a locker room culture that I think the players at this point are really enjoying,” Averbuch West says.

“Obviously, we had some cheaters in there,” Kitahata jokes. “Rose made sure that she held everyone accountable, made sure no one's looking it up on their phone.”

And Rose isn’t exactly a quiet participant. “She's in the meal room screaming out,” Kitahata recalls, imitating Rose’s clues: “Blank, blank, blank, blank. It's four words. Figure it out, guys!”

She’s equally serious about her teammates’ recovery. During a July road trip to Portland, Rose took Kitahata, still navigating her first professional season, along for a day of mental and physical recovery. They got coffee, browsed the shelves at Powell's Books, and headed to a sauna and cold plunge. A stickler for her recovery routine, Rose insisted they stay submerged up to their necks in the icy water for four minutes. “I'm like, ‘Rose. Rose, I'm freezing!’” Kitahata recalls. But Rose wouldn't budge. “I love that she always holds me accountable for my recovery.” They finished the day with sushi.

Rose is the teammate you want to run into when you’re having a bad day, because she notices, Kitahata says. “Even with a look, she's like, ‘Oh my god, what's wrong? Do we need to go get coffee?’ … It’s very rare to find someone of her stardom that is just so willing to give to the team.”

Rose has become more conscious of how much she'll miss these moments someday. “I'm such a painfully nostalgic person,” she says. “It's like, debilitating sometimes, honestly.”

It's the everyday moments with her teammates that seem to stick with her most. “I was thinking the other day, having moments in the locker room where we have this shared playlist that we're playing, and we're all dancing to it. And I was like, ‘How many grown adults get to dance with their friends in the locker room?’” she says. “It’s little things like that that it's so easy to take for granted, because we get to do it literally every single day, but something that I think is just so special.”

It's also clear how much she values the life she's built at Gotham, and how much she wants to keep being part of it. “I love my life off-field. I love my life on-field. I think we have such an incredible group of players and staff that we get to work with and learn from each day,” she says. “I just felt so content, but also challenged in a way that I felt like I was going to be happily growing.”

For Gotham, the move to Queens isn't just about having a place to call its own. It's also an opportunity to reach a bigger audience. Despite winning two NWSL championships in three seasons, Gotham has struggled to fill the stands in New Jersey consistently. But when the club hosted the Washington Spirit at Citi Field, home of the New York Mets, for its Queens Classic in July, the sellout crowd of 42,175 offered a glimpse of what might be possible across the street at Etihad Park.

The move isn't without its complications. Gotham has deep roots in New Jersey, and some longtime supporters are disappointed to see the club leave. “I was a little sad at first that the team was moving,” says Lindsey Smith, 37, a New Jersey resident and five-year season ticket holder. “But this is so much bigger than a location to me. As someone who wants to make women's sports accessible, I'm very excited for Gotham to move to Queens.”

Smith’s friend Krista Tonnesen, 37, has been attending Gotham matches regularly since 2021. An Astoria resident, that has meant long trips home after night games, sometimes with 40-minute waits between PATH trains. She's thrilled to have Gotham in her borough, and hopeful that the shorter trip will bring more fans into the stands. “The players and the team deserve better,” she says. “I really think moving to New York will turn that around.”

Rose says the atmosphere at the Queens Classic, where she scored the game's only goal, left an impression on her. “To think that we could be playing in front of a crowd and atmosphere like that, every single home game, I think it'll just add that much more to what we're building,” she says.

Back at Etihad Park, as we make our way through the unfinished concrete hallways, I ask Rose if she can picture what it'll be like when Gotham finally takes the field here. “I see the vision,” she says.

Amanda Lucci is the director of special projects at Women’s Health, where she works on multi-platform brand initiatives and social media strategy. She also leads the sports and athletes vertical, traveling to cover the Paris Olympics, Women’s World Cup, WNBA Finals, and NCAA Final Four for WH. She has nearly 15 years of experience writing, editing, and managing social media for national and international publications and is also a NASM-certified personal trainer. A proud native of Pittsburgh, PA, she is a graduate of Ohio University’s E.W. Scripps School of Journalism. Follow her on Instagram @alucci.

#5005. Want to Get Closer With Your Spouse? Take on a Wife Carrying Competition.

One couple‘s experience taking on a race that demands they shoulder each others’ burdens—literally.

DANNY AND KATIE Young are both active, but they’re not exactly the type of couple that stakes out dates on their calendar for the next Hyrox doubles race. Over the last few years, however, both went through separate fitness journeys. So on Christmas Day 2025 when Danny's older brother, Dylan, asked if they had heard of a wild event called wife carrying, they felt ready to take on a new challenge together.

Danny and Katie watched YouTube clips of the North American Wife Carrying Championship, an annual race held every October at the Sunday River Resort in Newry, Maine. It’s exactly what the name implies: a 278-yard race where husbands carry their wives on their backs. This is not the only one of its kind—similar events are staged around the globe and there’s a world championship in Finland—and competition isn’t limited to only married or man-woman couples. The Youngs scrolled through videos of couples running a steep obstacle course, jumping hurdles and sloshing through mud pits all while struggling to stay upright to the finish. The Youngs decided they wanted to take on the event in the coming year.

“One, because it’s so silly and ridiculous,” Katie tells Men’s Health. “Two, we used to live in Boston and we wanted to get back up to New England. The competition (held October 10 this year) was right around our 16th wedding anniversary, so we figured, what better way to celebrate?”

“We are seize the moment people–even if the moment is 10 months in advance,” Danny adds. “Signing up for the competition also felt like a natural celebratory culmination of what we’d done in the last year. We felt like: this is just the sort of thing we’ve been getting ready for. Let’s go do that, to applaud a really cool year of physical and health transformation.”

DANNY AND KATIE met when they were 14 years old, dated in high school, and got married shortly after graduating college. Now 38, they live on a hobby homestead outside of Asheville, NC. Their day jobs (Danny works as a manager at Trader Joe’s, Katie is a clinical supervisor for a community mental health clinic) and taking care of their daughter, Opal, and their small farm leaves little time for traditional training.

But Katie and Danny have navigated the challenges that come with maintaining fitness through both the busy schedules of everyday life and unexpected circumstances. In August of 2023, Katie had ankle surgery and was non-weight bearing for 14 weeks. She gained more than 100 pounds, which was difficult to manage on her 5’3’’ frame. By Thanksgiving of 2023, she started walking with a crutch, but she wasn’t able to return to her workouts (mostly hot yoga) until the summer of 2024. She loves power flows, hot HIIT classes, and hot lifts. “If it’s not over 100 degrees, I’m not interested,” Katie says. “I like that it’s balance, strength, and there’s a cardio element at times.” Twice a week turned to four times a week and then six; by the summer of 2026, she had lost 120 pounds.

Danny had been a CrossFit devotee in his 20s. His job at Trader Joe’s is very physical, picking up and carrying 20 to 50 pound boxes throughout the day. He had abandoned his gym habit in his late 20s; by 30, he struggled with back problems. He returned to the gym three years ago, alternating between calisthenics and foundational compound lifts. He trained twice a week, then three times, then every day. He, too, spent the last year transforming his body from a “dad bod” to one chiseled with muscles—and in top shape, stands at 6’3” and weighs 185 pounds.

But in June of this year, Danny injured his back during a deadlift session. He didn’t lift any weights for the rest of the summer. The couple were still committed to take on the wife carrying contest—but they needed to find a new way to train. They leaned on their active lifestyle on the homestead. They keep four goats, and Katie found that carrying two five-gallon buckets of water up and down the farm’s hills several times a week increased her strength. Katie often picked up one of the 80-pound goats to “escort her out of my garden, where she wants to eat my pumpkins.”

When Danny carried the buckets, he’d sometimes throw Katie on his back. He also carried her around the house and up and down the hills near the farm. They discussed which carry style might work best for them, trying piggyback, upside-down, and other methods. They never attempted a full 278-yard training run, instead opting for spontaneous moments of carrying when circumstances allowed.

“I really like the dual challenge of it, which appeals to both of us,” Danny says. “It’s like a test of a relationship alongside a physical feat. Silly, physical antics are part of our love language.”

RACE MORNING IN Newry, Maine brought clear, blue skies and strong winds. Almost five dozen couples warmed up by jogging up and down the rolling hills of the Sunday River Ski Resort ski slope, navigating sections of the 278-yard course and attempting the log hurdle in the carry position. That’s one of the two dry obstacles on the course; the other is a sand trap.

Danny and Katie watched other couples warming up and did some light stretching. Danny pumped out several sets of pushups as they waited. Hundreds of spectators lined the course.

“The community element seemed really fun,” Katie says afterward. “I can see why a lot of people watch it every year, or why people participate every year.” They met a couple who’d traveled from Utah to compete. Another pair had participated every year for the last 25, while others were in their 60s.

An M.C. announced each set of couples, who competed against one another in rounds of two and for time; the fastest two teams from the qualifying rounds would compete head-to-head in a final heat to determine the winner. A record 58 couples had signed up to compete.

Finally, it was their turn. Katie and Danny wished good luck to their co-competitors, and after several false starts as the MC adjusted his mic, the couples took off. Danny jogged as Katie held on to him utilizing a variation of the popular “Estonian carry,” where she was positioned upside-down, facing his back, using her arms to grip her own legs, which were wrapped over his shoulders like a yolk. The strength and balance she’d built through yoga showed as she held steady, despite Danny’s wobbles on the uneven terrain. She relied on core strength primarily, but also her arms and inner thighs to stay in position.

“It turns out that holding on to your own legs like an upside down human backpack while your partner runs uphill, clambers over a log hurdle, and wades through mud is much harder than it looks,” Katie says. “You’ve got to crunch up as tight as you can, squeeze your legs around your partner, wrap your arms into your own legs, and brace your core; all the while feeling your blood rushing to your head. I think the only reason I was able to do it is from the strength, endurance, and flexibility I’ve gained from my hot yoga practice. Plus, if you can tolerate exercising in a 105 degree room, what’s a little bit of mud?”

The pair didn’t talk much during the race itself. “Most of the race, the noise I was making was something along the lines of, ‘this is so much harder than I thought it was gonna be,’” Danny recalls.

Katie felt the early section had gone well, even though she admittedly couldn’t see anything but the landscape passing below her. “And then, all of a sudden, I can see the ground is moving a lot slower, and Danny's panting and saying how hard it is,” she says.

Still, they kept moving, navigating a steep uphill and then descending toward the mud pit, the course’s final challenge also known as the “Widow-Maker.” Katie had photographed the mud pit prior to the race and texted her friends with the caption, “My fate.”

Danny stepped into the mud hole, navigating the first big step downward. He wobbled. Katie, sensing his unsteadiness, didn’t want to be dropped on her neck and head. “So I abandoned ship,” she says. She threw herself into the cold, wet mud, which filled her mouth and covered her eyes and nose. “It was a pretty solid belly flop,” she added. The muddy water coated her body, officially making the Youngs the first full “mud drop” out of the 30-plus couples that had competed up to that point.

“As I felt her coming off of my back, I thought, ‘where is she going?’” Danny adds afterward.

Katie stood up, wet and laughing so hard she needed a moment to steady herself. The plan had been for Katie to carry Danny across the finish line–but after they cleared the mud pit and stepped onto dry land, he was so wet and she was laughing so hard, she couldn't quite grip him. She jumped onto him, piggy-back, as they emerged from the mud pit and jogged the final yards to the finish line. Just before crossing, she hopped off his back and picked him up, carrying Danny across the finish, laughing as the crowd cheered.

The winning couple was celebrating their 10th anniversary, and their children joined them in crossing the finish line. Most of the competitors stayed through the full two-hour event, as did the spectators, watching the award ceremony and cheering for the various additional contests (best dressed couple and other superlatives.) The winning couple received the wife's weight in beer and five times her weight in cash. Volunteers handed out beers to the entrants. Katie changed out of her wet clothes before they joined his parents, Dylan and his wife, Kristen, and Katie and Danny’s 8-year-old daughter, Opal.

In carrying Danny across the finish, Katie wanted to reinforce what Opal had learned: “Mommy and Daddy are both strong and capable, and there’s no difference,” Katie says.

OVERALL, THE BIGGEST takeaway from the race had little to do with their time or the physical challenge that came with hauling one another across the course. Their relationship, they say, has always been strong—and attempting a fun, unique competition reinforced the tenets of their partnership: fun, laughter, strength, and supporting each other.

Danny suggested they enter again next year, “which I’m definitely up for, competition-wise,” Katie says. “I want to do many ridiculous competitions with you,” Danny adds. “Our relationship quality has been great, and this added another fun adventure.”

After the race, Danny considered how he might approach training for their next attempt. “If we were to run it back, I would definitely add hill sprints to my routine,” he says. “I should have prepared for the fact that the race is very short and very intense. Interval sprints and sled pushing would have been more aligned with the demands of the competition.”

But nothing, in his opinion, is better than replicating the real challenge of the event: carrying a partner and working together. “Spend as much time as possible with your wife on your back,” he says. “It's kooky and fun, and there's nothing else that quite mimics the awkwardness of two spouses becoming one athlete.”

#5004. Initia confirms second exploit in 3 weeks, $3.27M stolen in latest attack

Initia said an attacker exploited compromised cloud credentials to move $3.27 million in crypto assets, with about $2.16 million remaining frozen on its halted network.

Layer-1 blockchain network Initia confirmed Wednesday that it has suffered its second security exploit in less than three weeks, with an attacker stealing about $3.27 million in crypto assets after compromising the platform’s cloud infrastructure.

On Wednesday, Initia said the attacker gained unauthorized access on Monday through a command injection attack involving a compromised Google Cloud Platform key. The company said the breach exposed environment variables, allowing the attacker to access Initia’s GitHub and cloud accounts and obtain additional credentials.

According to Initia, the attacker accessed Anvil, a platform hosting rollups, and validated fraudulent Inter-Blockchain Communication (IBC) withdrawals from five rollups, releasing assets held in the network’s escrow accounts.

Initia said about $2.16 million of the stolen assets remain frozen on its halted network. The attacker sold assets on Initia’s decentralized exchange and withdrew 13,400 TIA, 8,000 USDC.inj and 597,000 Noble USDC. No INIT tokens were withdrawn.

Initia halted its layer-1 on Tuesday and said it intends to restore the backing of affected assets. The blockchain said recovery efforts are ongoing with its law enforcement and security partners.

The incident follows an Oct. 23 exploit that saw an attacker drain about $2.7 million after exploiting a vulnerability in Initia’s MoveVM script cache. This allowed malicious code to bypass security checks and create unbacked token balances that were subsequently bridged out of the network.

#5003. Nvidia Sheds $430B in Two Days as Jensen Huang Faces Calls to Resign

Nvidia Sheds $430B in Two Days as Jensen Huang Faces Calls to Resign

Nvidia has lost about $430 billion in market value in two days, as pressure grows in Washington over CEO Jensen Huang's ties to a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly concluded was a Chinese spy.

Nvidia shares closed down 5.5% at $168.89 on Tuesday, following a 4.3% drop on Monday, according to Yahoo! Finance. That leaves the stock down about 9.5% since Friday's close, wiping out roughly $430 billion in market value, according to Yahoo! Finance data. The sell-off, which saw shares slide as much as 8% on Monday, has wiped out all of Nvidia's gains for 2026. The shares were up 3.8% at $175.23 in premarket trading early on Wednesday.

The independent members of Nvidia's board have retained Joon Kim, a former acting U.S. Attorney for the Southern District of New York, and his firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to carry out the investigation it announced on Sunday, the company said on Tuesday.

A new statement from the independent members of the NVIDIA Board: https://t.co/uSFDhiohG8

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 10, 2026

Sen. Kevin Cramer, a North Dakota Republican on the Senate Banking Committee, called on Huang to step down, telling Politico late Tuesday that the risk of extortion was too great. "The potential for extortion is so tremendous that something as sensitive as Nvidia chips, I just think he's got to go," Cramer said, adding that the board's internal investigation was "fine, but not nearly enough."

Hours earlier, former National Economic Council director Larry Kudlow had urged Huang to resign on his Fox Business show. "Step down, Mr. Huang, right now," Kudlow said, adding that he was "not here to indict" Huang but that he should step aside while the investigations take place.

Cramer's committee has jurisdiction over bills that would curb Nvidia chip sales to China, including the AI Overwatch Act and the Chip Security Act, which are being considered for inclusion in the annual defense spending bill, Politico reported.

Senate Democratic leader Chuck Schumer, meanwhile, accused President Donald Trump of failing to act after being briefed on the relationship. "What a dereliction of duty," Schumer said on the Senate floor on Tuesday, according to CNBC. "He's supposed to protect America, not China, not his friends."

Rep. John Moolenaar (R-MI), who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said he wanted to know whether Huang shared privileged information with Alice Huang and whether he was influenced to lobby against chip export restrictions. "If it becomes less of an investigation, more of damage control, that's not going to work," Moolenaar told CNBC. House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast told ABC News he was prepared to subpoena Huang unless he responded by Wednesday to a request for a closed-door briefing on November 18, the day after Nvidia reports quarterly earnings.

Not everyone in Washington wants the scandal to dominate. Rep. Jay Obernolte (R-CA), co-chair of the House AI task force, told CNBC that a personal scandal should not "interfere" with the policy debate over chip exports to China.

Trump has said the FBI is looking into the matter, and that Huang told him he had no idea the woman was a spy.

Alice Huang, who also goes by Huang Le and is not related to Jensen Huang, worked in banking and investment management in China before becoming the controlling shareholder of Singapore-based Megaspeed in March 2023, according to a Reuters review of corporate records and filings.

Megaspeed, which Nvidia lists as a cloud partner, has drawn U.S. scrutiny over whether it helped Chinese firms obtain export-controlled Nvidia chips, but there has been no public finding that it broke the rules, Reuters reported.

China has denied the intelligence allegations, and Reuters said it could not independently verify them. George Chen, a partner at consultancy The Asia Group, told the news agency that Jensen Huang "may also be viewed as a potential victim" if he was targeted without knowing who she was.

#5001. SEC enforcement actions fall 24%, crypto cases in spotlight

The SEC filed 348 actions in fiscal 2026, down from 456 a year earlier. Cases included alleged Bitcoin mining fraud and an FTX audit settlement.

The US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) filed 348 enforcement actions in fiscal 2026, down 23.7% from 456 a year earlier.

Its annual enforcement results included cases involving alleged crypto fraud and failures in FTX’s audits.

In its Tuesday release, the SEC said it had charged three purported crypto trading platforms and four investment clubs in an alleged scheme that defrauded investors of at least $14 million. In a separate case, the agency accused the founder of a Bitcoin mining business of misappropriating $48.5 million after raising more than $95.6 million from about 6,400 investors.

In April, the SEC settled charges against Francis Decker, the Prager Metis partner who led FTX’s audits, over failures to meet professional auditing standards.

In February, a jury found Ismael Zarco Sanchez liable for securities fraud and registration violations over his role in the $300 million CryptoFX Ponzi scheme. In July, a court ordered Michael Noble to pay $1.07 million in a case over his role promoting BitConnect’s crypto lending program.

Fiscal 2026 was SEC Chair Paul Atkins’ first full fiscal year in office. The 348 enforcement actions were the agency’s fewest since 1991, according to a Reuters review of SEC records. Standalone actions fell to 235 from 303 a year earlier.

The decline in crypto enforcement was already visible in 2025, when the SEC filed 13 crypto-related actions, down from 33 in 2024, according to Cornerstone Research. Five of the 13 were filed before former Chair Gary Gensler left office in January 2025.

The SEC said its new enforcement analytics office was using “blockchain and financial-flow analysis” to detect potential misconduct earlier.

#5000. Do You Need a Vitamin B12 Supplement If You’re Taking a GLP-1?

Here’s why you don’t want vitamin B12 levels getting too low.

WHILE GLP-1 MEDS have been game changers for weight management, they’ve also introduced some challenges for people looking to lose weight. They reduce appetite and help you feel satisfied sooner, but that can make it more challenging to get all the nutrients your body needs.

One of the nutrients GLP-1 users may get less of is Vitamin B12. On social media, people discussing life on GLP-1s often bring up eating more foods high in B12 or recommending B12 supplements when complaints of side effects like fatigue and headaches come up.

Here’s what you need to know about vitamin B12 and GLP-1s along with how to make sure you’re getting enough.

VITAMIN B12, ALSO known as cobalamin, is an essential vitamin that cannot be produced by the body. It must come from food or supplements, says internist Jennifer Chung, MD.

Vitamin B12 supports several important bodily functions. It’s involved in the production of red blood cells, creation of DNA, and maintenance of a healthy nervous system. The demand for vitamin B12—the recommended daily intake of vitamin B12 for most adults is 2.4 micrograms—doesn’t go away even when you’re consuming fewer calories.

WHEN VITAMIN B12 levels drop too low, the effects can range from fatigue and weakness to more serious symptoms. Dr. Chung says this might involve anemia, which is a lack of healthy red blood cells to carry oxygen throughout the body, tingling or numbness in the hands and feet, and problems with balance or memory. If vitamin B12 deficiency persists, you may run the risk of permanent nerve damage.

TAKING A GLP-1 doesn’t automatically mean you’ll develop a B12 deficiency. It’s more of an indirect effect.

“GLP-1 medications are not known to reduce absorption of vitamin B12, unlike metformin, which interferes with the binding of vitamin B12 in the gastrointestinal tract,” explains Dr. Chung. “Instead, nutrient deficiencies with GLP-1 medications arise from a combination of eating less food, eating less dietary variety, delayed gastric emptying, and rapid weight loss.”

The common side effects people experience while taking a GLP-1—nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and constipation—may also contribute to not getting enough vitamins like B12.

DOCTORS SAY A vitamin B12 supplement is worth considering for GLP-1 users, but it’s not a requirement.

“Several medical societies recommend that GLP-1 users consider supplementation with vitamin D, calcium, B12, and a multivitamin to reduce micronutrient deficiencies, but there is no universal recommendation,” says Dr. Chung.

Todd Worley, MD, bariatric surgeon and obesity medicine specialist at Houston Methodist Willowbrook Hospital, says the primary goal is to get enough of this essential nutrient through food.

B12 is found in a variety of animal-based foods, including beef, poultry, salmon, tuna, trout, eggs, clams, and dairy products. You can also find a comprehensive list of B12-rich foods here.

If a reduced appetite is making you unable to stomach enough food, then it’s time to consider a supplement. Another reason to consider a supplement is if you follow a plant-based or vegan diet.

Jacqueline Vernarelli, PhD, nutrition scientist at Sacred Heart University, says that unfortified plant foods aren’t a reliable source of the nutrient since vitamin B12 is primarily in animal-based foods. As a result, she recommends regularly incorporating B12-fortified foods, such as certain breakfast cereals and plant-based milks, to help meet your daily intake.

AGE AND SOME gastrointestinal conditions can also make it harder for your body to absorb the vitamin. “Conditions that reduce stomach acid increase with age, and stomach acid is needed to release vitamin B12 from protein in food so it can be absorbed,” says Dr. Chung. Guys might start feeling the effects of vitamin B12 deficiency in their 50s.

A B12 DEFICIENCY can be difficult to recognize because symptoms often develop gradually and may overlap with other issues. For example, you may attribute fatigue to not eating enough overall when it’s actually because you’re low on B12.

Dr. Chung recommends talking with the physician who prescribed your GLP-1 about any changes you’re feeling. They may recommend blood tests to check your B12 levels.

If blood tests confirm a B12 deficiency, your doctor may recommend dietary changes or a supplement.

Oral B12 supplements in capsule, tablet, or liquid form are often the first line of defense, with a common daily dose of 1,000 micrograms, says Dr. Chung. The reason supplements have larger doses than the recommended daily intake is because the body only absorbs a portion of the vitamin. Intramuscular B12 injections are also an option, but Dr. Worley says those are usually reserved for folks with absorption problems or more significant B12 deficiencies.

The right dose and form of B12 supplementation will depend on your individual needs. There’s no one-size-fits-all approach. Above all, Dr. Chung recommends being proactive and planning meals with enough B12 sources and keeping tabs on any signs of potential deficiencies. Losing weight shouldn’t mean sacrificing good health.

Andi Breitowich is a freelance writer who covers health, fitness, relationships, beauty, and smart living. She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. Her work has appeared in Women's Health, POPSUGAR, Food & Wine, What To Expect, Cosmopolitan, Men's Health, and elsewhere. As a former collegiate pole vaulter, she loves all things fitness and has yet to meet a group workout class she doesn't like.

#4999. Kraken Parent Payward Brings 200 UK and EU Stocks Onchain as xStocks

Kraken Parent Payward Brings 200 UK and EU Stocks Onchain as xStocks

Payward, the parent company of crypto exchange Kraken, has tokenized more than 200 UK and European stocks through its xStocks platform, it announced on Wednesday.

The first batch covers some of the most actively traded companies listed in the UK and the European Union, with more to be added on a rolling basis. Like other xStocks, each token is backed 1:1 by the underlying share, which is bought and held by a regulated custodian for as long as the token trades, according to Payward.

Payward's @xStocksFi is expanding beyond US tokenized equities.

In partnership with @GTNglobal, more than 200 of the UK and EU’s largest equities are now tokenized by xStocks, including HSBC, Shell, LVMH and Airbus.

The first international launch, with more markets to follow.…

The tokens are available to trade on Kraken and through other platforms in the xStocks Alliance, and on blockchains where xStocks already trade, including Solana, Ethereum and TON. They are offered in more than 110 markets, but remain unavailable to investors in the UK.

"A portfolio should not have borders or trading hours," said Mark Greenberg, Payward's chief commercial officer. "Tokenizing every other international market is the next step."

The launch takes xStocks past 1,500 tokenized stocks, ETFs and IPOs. The platform holds more than $1.1 billion in assets, has processed over $45 billion in total transaction volume, and has more than 550,000 holders, Payward said.

The new tokens are the first delivered under Payward's partnership with GTN, a brokerage infrastructure firm that handles execution and custody of the underlying shares. When the two partnered in July, Payward said it planned to start with Hong Kong-listed stocks; hundreds of Hong Kong listings will now follow in the coming weeks, the company said.

The expansion also delivers on part of Payward's partnership with the London Stock Exchange, announced in September, under which Payward agreed to tokenize the 100 largest London-listed companies. Subject to regulatory approval, the exchange intends to list xStocks and begin trading them on LSE 24, its planned near-24-hour trading venue, in 2027.

Payward has been drawing interest from traditional exchanges. Nasdaq agreed to invest $100 million in the company at a $21 billion valuation in September, building on a March partnership to bring tokenized stocks to market in 2027, while Deutsche Börse bought a $200 million stake in April.

The company has also been expanding through acquisitions, agreeing to buy derivatives exchange Bitnomial for $550 million and Lithuania's Saldo Bank as part of a push into the EU.

#4997. Stock ETF buyers pounce on dip as Bitcoin funds bleed $1.6 billion

US spot Bitcoin ETFs have lost $1.6 billion in November, while stock funds attracted billions of dollars from investors buying the dip.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded $95 million in net outflows on Tuesday as investors poured billions of dollars into stock funds during a market pullback.

Tuesday’s withdrawals brought net outflows from US spot Bitcoin (BTC) ETFs to roughly $1.6 billion since the start of November, according to SoSoValue data.

Bitcoin ETF outflows eased for a third straight session, while stock funds accounted for all 17 of the top US-listed ETFs by inflows over five trading sessions, according to Bloomberg ETF analyst Eric Balchunas.

US-listed ETFs attracted roughly $12 billion per day over those five sessions, with all 17 of the top funds by inflows investing in equities, Balchunas said on X on Tuesday.

The biggest beneficiaries included Vanguard’s S&P 500 ETF (VOO), Invesco’s Nasdaq-100 ETF (QQQ) and BlackRock’s iShares Core S&P 500 ETF (IVV).

US-listed ETFs ranked by one-week net inflows, led by equity funds. Source: Eric Balchunas

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $78 million in net withdrawals Tuesday, its eighth straight trading session of outflows. The fund recorded about $1.73 billion in net outflows over the streak, according to Farside Investors.

Morgan Stanley’s Bitcoin Trust ETF (MSBT) attracted $20 million Tuesday, although the inflows were insufficient to offset broader losses.

Daily net outflows from Bitcoin ETFs outflows slowed from $430 million Thursday and $412 million Friday to $201 million Monday and $95 million Tuesday, but broader market indicators remained weak, according to CryptoQuant.

CryptoQuant’s Bitcoin Bull Score Index dropped to 20 on Monday from 90 on Oct. 13, signaling “extremely bearish” conditions, according to a Tuesday report seen by Cointelegraph.

#4996. Your 12-Week Training Plan for Burning Fat and Building Muscle After 40

No gym needed—just at-home workouts designed for the body you have now.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

At 40, work, kids, and everyone else’s demands have a way of pushing training to the back burner. If this sounds familiar, you don’t need a complex routine; you need a plan that fits your life. That’s why Men’s Health teamed with Andrew Heffernan, C.S.C.S., for this 12-week training program, Muscle After 40 at Home, because your healthiest, strongest days are still ahead of you. The good news: you can get there without leaving the house. No pricey gym membership needed, just a few dumbbells, consistency, and some serious grit.

Over 12 weeks, you’ll develop the skills to kickstart your longevity and see real results so you can stay strong in your 40s, 50s, and beyond. In just three months, you’ll learn how to spot good pain versus bad pain, learn the moves to avoid injury, and get the energy you need while improving health markers your physician has been nagging you about. You’ll also build strength, athleticism, and a toolkit to maintain your body for the long haul.

Forty is not the new twenty, but that doesn't mean that you can’t reap the same gains you had in your youth. Over the next 12 weeks, you’ll learn exercises to boost your muscle mass while avoiding injury—heavy barbell squats and deadlifts, upright rows, behind-the-neck presses and Olympic lifts won’t take center stage here. Instead, you’ll focus on joint-friendly workouts like goblet squats, lunge variations, dumbbell Romanian deadlifts, pushup variations, and core work.

You’ll start each workout with a mobility warmup and quick explosive moves like skips and shuffle steps. Speed fades with age, so these quick warmups will help you maintain your agility for years to come.

In addition to rigorous training plans, you’ll also get a whole-life health approach to training, complete with nutrition guidance, tips for better sleep, smart cardio, an actionable plan for keeping your gains once the 12 weeks are up, plus an illustrated glossary of nearly 60 exercises.

Traditional training models focus on reps; Muscle After 40 at Home is effort-based. Instead of mandating 10 reps per exercise, each set is assigned an effort level on a scale of 1-10. With only light, medium, and heavy dumbbells to choose from, every workout remains challenging whatever your starting strength. You’ll begin with an overhead-press test to help you pick the right weights. This book also comes with a built-in workout journal to help you track your progress with each set.

This training plan keeps it simple. You’ll need:

Stagnation comes at a cost. According to a recent article published through Harvard Medical School, starting around age 30, you begin losing muscle at nearly 1 percent a year if you remain inactive. That gradual loss is called age-related sarcopenia. Over time, this sets the stage for a cascade of health issues: more fat, less function and mobility, and a host of illnesses, including heart disease and even serious, life-threatening injury.

With Muscle After 40 at Home, you’ll pack on muscle, avoid injury, and see real results in a matter of weeks.

#4995. Fashion Maverick Christopher John Rogers Unveils Bold New Carpets for The Rug Company

For Christopher John Rogers, the magic of a garment lies in its making. “I was thinking about preserving what happens in the studio—draping, cutting, chalk lines, thread—and the mistakes, smears, and imperfections that are left by the hand,” notes the Louisiana-born, New York–based fashion designer. That ethos became the departure point for his new collection, The Studio by Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company. Conceived in partnership with the brand’s creative director, Will Cooper, the line builds upon The Rug Company’s history of collaborations with fashion greats such as Alexander McQueen, Diane von Furstenberg, and Vivienne Westwood.

Marker Test rug showcased in Will Cooper's apartment

Another rug from The Studio By Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company collection in Cooper's living area

Spanning a range of flatweaves and plush piles, crafted using hand-knotted and woven techniques, Rogers’s six designs reference the abstract paintings of Morris Louis and Ellsworth Kelly in addition to his own archival runway looks and creative process. “The patterns came from something as simple as my marker tests on paper,” he says, recalling how he reinterpreted those scribbles, which date to his 2022 pre-fall runway collection, in silk and wool. “I’ve always loved the idea that something accidental can become the very thing you want to preserve.”

Whereas Paintstroke evokes a wet stripe of pigment, Chalk Line juxtaposes raised cross-hatchings against a neutral ground. Mixing faded primary colors with warm earth tones, the palette channels Rogers’s signature hues through a subdued lens, the construction of each piece adding a sense of patina. “We wanted the rugs to feel like they could be from any decade; they could be vintage pieces,” says Cooper, who was introduced to Rogers by past brand collaborator Farrow & Ball. “They reflect Christopher’s evolution and traces of the many hands involved in their making—histories that ultimately become part of the home.”

#4994. ECB warns legislative delays could postpone digital euro launch

ECB Executive Board member Piero Cipollone warned EU lawmakers that delays in finalizing digital euro legislation could push back its potential 2029 rollout.

European Central Bank (ECB) Executive Board member Piero Cipollone called for European Union negotiators to reach a deal on digital euro legislation by year-end to keep a possible 2029 launch on track.

Speaking at the European Parliament’s Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs on Tuesday, Cipollone said adoption of the regulation, part of the EU’s Single Currency Package, would give the ECB the legal certainty it needs to complete preparations.

“A delay in adoption could instead postpone the introduction of the digital euro,” Cipollone said, warning that a later launch could deepen Europe’s dependence on foreign payment providers and make it harder for European companies to compete.

The ECB aims to be ready for a potential first issuance in 2029 if the EU adopts the regulation this year. Its Governing Council will decide whether and when to issue the digital euro after the legislation passes.

The European Parliament’s economic affairs committee approved its position on the digital euro legislation in June in a 43–14 vote. Parliament adopted its negotiating position in July, clearing the way for talks with EU member states.

The ECB is preparing a 12-month pilot set to begin in the second half of 2027. Payment providers and selected merchants will test a beta version of the digital euro.

The warning comes as the ECB prepares for a leadership change. President Christine Lagarde announced in October that she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, ahead of the planned pilot.

#4993. The Best Travel Backpacks for Women in 2026, Tested by Editors

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

The right travel backpack helps you master the art of packing light, so you aren’t hauling unnecessary weight to Terminal B. It has enough space for your flight essentials—laptop, passport, water bottle, charging cables—and is comfortable enough to strap on for hours. Whether you need an upgrade to complement your new carry-on, are investing in a pack to carry you through your next adventure, or simply looking for a great Christmas gift, here’s what to consider before you shop.

Below, see the best travel backpacks for women to take on your next adventure.

Topo’s Rover Pack is truly multifunctional, and that’s why we’ve dubbed it our best overall travel backpack. Its thoughtful design, roomy compartments, and casual look make it great for someone who’s always on the move, whether they’re going to the gym, commuting to work, or packing for their next trip.

The backpack has a 20-liter capacity, spread across a large main compartment and a zippered pocket on the front. There’s also a padded internal laptop sleeve, two adjustable water bottle pockets, and more. Plus, it’s made with dual side compression and spacer mesh shoulder straps. While it packs a ton, the bag is midsize enough that it can be used as an everyday bag, too.

The backpack’s main compartment is designed with a drawstring closure that can be cinched closed, and then secured using the two exterior straps with buckle closures. This adds some reassurance that whatever you’ve packed inside won’t spill out, or be at risk of getting stolen on hectic travel days.

We love that this bag comes in more than 15 fun color combinations that will stand out from the rest of the plain backpacks. Bonus points that it’s made from durable recycled nylon.

Most of the best travel backpacks will cost you at least $100—and sometimes more. But the Matein Carry-On Travel Backpack is the exception. This bag is designed with the same features as the other backpacks in this guide for a fraction of the price—under $40. It has more than seven compartments, including a hidden pocket with two ventilation holes that can be used to store shoes up to a size 12 or dirty laundry.

Inside you’ll find a padded neoprene pocket that can fit laptops up to 17 inches. The pocket closes with two zippers that meet, allowing you to add a lock to that particular compartment if you want to deter thieves. One feature we particularly love (in addition to its multiple compartments) is that it’s designed with comfort in mind. The part of the backpack that lies on your lower back when worn is made with extra padding to avoid discomfort, and its straps are padded, too.

A chest strap with a buckle closure would have made this backpack perfect to help distribute some of its weight (40 liters can feel quite heavy to some!), but it does come with a luggage strap you can use to rest it on your carry-on or checked luggage while you’re on your way to your destination.

We also love the hidden pocket in the back to store your most valuable items, like money, your passport, or other documents. To pack the main compartment, you can open the bag up like a clamshell, which makes it easy to store your clothing and other travel essentials in a way that makes the most out of the space you have.

The North Face Borealis is a classic backpack that’s been around for decades—25 years to be exact, and many people who own the cult-favorite bag will tell you they’ve had one for years. The Borealis was originally designed to be used on the trails, but it’s so spacious and easy to organize that it has gained a reputation as a commuter and travel backpack as well.

Assistant social media editor Isabel McMahon says she’s been personally using this bag for years because it’s so comfortable and durable. “The padded back is essential for a long travel day,” she says. McMahon also says the bag’s internal laptop compartment (which fits a computer up to 16 inches) is “next-level,” as it sits a few inches above the bottom of the bag to protect your electronics from accidental drops. The inside also features other small pockets for a phone, documents, and other tiny valuables.

We particularly love that this backpack is designed from a recycled nylon and polyester fabric blend that’s both water-repellent and hard to rip. That’s one of the reasons the bag is so durable—it can truly last decades when cared for. The iconic bungee cord system in the front adds a stylish note that makes this backpack stand apart from other options, but the cords are also functional, too. These can be tightened to hold other essentials, like a water bottle, travel blanket, or jacket.

McMahon’s only complaint about this bag is that there are a lot of straps and clips, which can sometimes get in the way and accidentally snag onto other items, she says. Still, she raves that it makes the perfect bag for a weekend trip at 27 liters.

Another cool feature of this bag is that it’s made with The North Face’s FlexVent system, certified by the American Chiropractic Association. This means the shoulder straps and back panel are designed to contour to the curvature of your spine, so that the bag is easier to hold and there’s less of a chance of you experiencing soreness or fatigue.

Read More: Best Travel Essentials for Women

As denoted by its name, the Triple-Zip Backpack has three main zipper compartments: the main center compartment, the large front pocket, and an external padded laptop pocket that can fit up to a 16-inch laptop. Each pocket is strategically placed, so you have easy access to your laptop, hydration, and any small essentials without having to dig into the main compartment.

Lululemon’s Tech Canvas material features reflective details and is made from recycled polyester, which is water-repellent, making this backpack a great choice for travel. It also has a trolley-compatible sleeve to slip over your luggage while making your way through the airport.

We love the attention to detail built into the thick padded straps—they sit closer together on the back to evenly distribute the load and have an adjustable sternum strap. Plus, load-lifter straps secure the bag to your upper body and the cushy back panel provides lumbar support.

Hikers or outdoor adventurers will enjoy traveling with Cotopaxi’s Allpa 28L Travel Pack. It’s lightweight, made of rip-proof fabric, and easy to clean. Additionally, it’s waterproof, which also makes it a solid choice for those that will face a variety of weather conditions. The Allpa is the backpack I traveled with when I trekked through the Amazon in Ecuador for two days. While there, I regularly had to lay the backpack on the ground for rest and other reasons, so it did get quite dirty. However, I loved that all it took was a good wipe-down to make the bag look brand-new again. I love that it can be easily cleaned using a wet towel, which makes it a solid choice for trips where it can get soiled easily.

Even better? The backpack is made with 100 percent recycled fabric, but it doesn’t skimp on durability. This bag was regularly grazed by tree branches during my hikes or dragged across a floor in my quest to unpack it, and it didn’t experience any rips. It’s made with many of the same features as the other backpacks in this guide—different-sized compartments, a water bottle mesh pocket on the side, and a rollerbag sleeve for resting it on luggage—but it also features four webbing loops along each corner of the bag, which are perfect for attaching gear, like a jacket, clip-on speaker, or a flashlight.

My only gripe with the backpack is that it has so many compartments I lost track of where I put things sometimes. But now that I’ve used it several times, that’s no longer an issue. It’s something first-time users can definitely experience, though.

While the Osprey Fairview is a much pricier buy than the Matein, it’s a lot more supportive (and long-term durable), making it well worth the splurge. Meg Wubbenhorst, a travel expert who has traveled to five continents and over 40 countries, says she regularly used the 40-liter backpack during her 20 months of solo travel, even though she originally thought it looked too small.

“I was pleasantly surprised at the roominess—I sometimes felt like Mary Poppins when unpacking, with ample room for all of my clothes, shoes (no more than 3 pairs!), and various travel accessories,” she says.

From the compression straps on the outside and inside of the bag to the countless mesh compartments and laptop sleeve, this bag makes it easy to get organized, but it’s the backpack’s support features that are impressive. The Osprey bag is made with an adjustable sternum strap that comes with a whistle (particularly useful for adventurers), and a hip belt that helps evenly distribute the bag’s weight to remove strain on your back and shoulders.

It’s also built with Osprey’s proprietary suspension system, which is designed to keep the backpack close to your body, while spreading its load across the entire back panel. This prevents the bag’s weight from pulling you back, so that the bag moves with you as you travel and not against you.

Additionally, whenever you do get tired of carrying this workhorse on your back, you can use either of the convenient carrying handles on its top and left sides. Wubbenhorst also says that she particularly likes that this bag passes most airlines’ carry-on policy, even when it’s stuffed to the gills.

The Kroser Laptop Backpack is a great option for those looking for a backpack for daily use, or those who regularly go on overnight stays. It’s also a great pick for those who prefer to pack their travel backpack with just the essentials—documents, travel blanket, etc. It only holds 37 liters and features multiple pockets for easy organization, including compartments for a laptop, water bottle, pens, and valuables.

Alexandra Dubakova, head of marketing at FreeTour.com, a website for booking local tours, personally likes the Kroser backpack because it’s designed with an aqua blue lining that’s more unique than the usual basic black or brown, and, in her opinion, a lot more functional, too. “That pop of color makes it so much easier to spot my stuff inside the bag,” she says. “I’m always digging around to find things, so having that brighter interior is super handy.” It also features a collapsible steel frame box opening that can give you a bird’s-eye view of what’s in the bag.

On the downside, she doesn’t love that the zippers are bulky and visible, since they make the pockets an easy target for thieves. The bag allows you to secure the two handles with a button once it’s zippered up, though, adding an extra level of security that can provide some extra reassurance that your items will remain safe.

If you’re looking for a backpack that easily fits under your airplane seat, Away’s Active Backpack is a solid choice. It’s also a great option for those that will be taking a backpack on their daily excursions once they arrive at their destination.

First, style-wise the bag is very chic. It’s designed for the outdoors, but it’s stylish enough to take along on your metro adventures without fear it’ll clash with your outfit and be an eyesore in photos. It’s also made from abrasion-resistant material that holds up against the elements—so there’s no problem using this one in rain or snow. While on a trip to Boston, I used this bag during an unexpected flurry of snow, and the bag quickly dried without incident to any of my belongings.

I also found it to be super lightweight. I wore it on the subway to go catch my bus to Boston from New York, and I had no complaints. I packed it with quite a few things—my laptop, some full-sized beauty products, and two days of outfits—and I never felt that the bag was so heavy that I had to put it down to rest.

The Away bag features several compartments, but my favorite is the hidden pocket in the back behind the bag’s straps. I tend to get paranoid that my wallet will get swiped, but storing it back there reassured me that even the savviest thief would likely not find it. Its thoughtful design and lightweight feel make it the perfect everyday bag for longer trips, but it can be used on its own for overnight stays or weekend trips.

What stands out about this Calpak backpack is that it doubles as a duffel bag if you get tired of carrying it on your back. The backpack comes with a removable longer strap you can use to hold the duffel on a shoulder. It also comes with carrying handles—one on the top and one on the side—so you can hold it in whatever orientation feels most comfortable.

Like most of the other backpacks in this guide, it features a ton of pockets, including a zippered water bottle pocket on its left side, a laptop sleeve, an internal mesh pocket, two small zippered compartments in the front of the bag, among several others strategically placed inside the bag. The Terra is designed with a clamshell opening, which makes it relatively easy to pack, and two compression straps in the main compartment designed to hold your contents in place.

Because of its 26-liter capacity, on its own, it’s spacious enough for three to four days of travel, but keep in mind that when it’s packed to the brim, we did find that it was a little tough to get its trolley sleeve over a luggage handle. An extra feature we would have loved to see is a waist strap. It does come with a sternum strap, but a waist strap would make this backpack even more comfortable to carry. This bag does get bonus points for being made from 27 recycled plastic bottles. Sustainability is always a plus.

When shopping for a travel backpack, most brands describe how much it can hold in liters. For longer trips over three days, a larger capacity bag—40 liters or more—is the better choice.

For shorter, weekend-long excursions, you can go for backpacks with a 25- to 30-liter capacity. If you’re using your travel backpack in addition to roller luggage, or as a day bag during your travels, you can go even smaller with 18 to 22 liters. “[Too much] weight can be a bit uncomfortable when traveling around Europe and other places, so I try to stay below 40 liters,” says Tamia Ramsey, a travel expert who has been to over 40 countries and founder of the budget travel blog, Poor in a Private Plane.

Ramsey recommends backpacks with four or more compartments, since they make it easy to organize clothing, electronics, valuables, and other travel essentials. She also finds a pocket in the front particularly useful for storing things you may need on the fly, like a pen.

Other features that can make a travel backpack even more functional are a water bottle holder, a shoe or dirty laundry compartment, and a pocket that isn’t too visible for protecting your wallet, passport, and other valuables from thieves.

Finally, if you want to offset the weight of your bag, you can seek options with sternum and chest straps. These straps typically buckle along the front of your body to redistribute the backpack’s weight and take some of the strain off your back and shoulders.

Travel backpacks can open in a ton of different ways, but some of the most popular options include clamshell, U-shaped, and top-loading designs. A clamshell design opens up like a book, similar to a suitcase. These are easy to pack because they give you a bird’s-eye view of what’s in the bag. Others are top-loading, which means they open up at the top, allowing you to pack from the bottom of the bag up. Finally, backpacks with U-shaped openings are made with a zipper that goes around three sides of the backpack. These provide a wider opening than top-loading backpacks. That allows you to access things quickly without having to lay your bag down on the ground, like you would a backpack with a clamshell opening.

The type of opening you choose depends on your preference, but ones with a clamshell opening are typically the easiest to organize and pack because you get such a great view of what’s inside.

Their intel helped me evaluate the following travel backpacks for women on packability, carry comfort, design, durability, and accessibility.

#4992. Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute: What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

💵 What Anthropic’s $1.4 Trillion IPO Means for Crypto

Several key new details for Anthropic’s pending IPO were released on Tuesday. The IPO price range was set at $70 to $78 a share, selling 825 million Class A shares to raise about $64.4 billion (at the top of the range). Pricing is expected November 18, with trading starting November 19 on Nasdaq under ANT. Roughly 20% of the deal is set aside for retail, which is about double what a book this size usually gives individual investors.

Notably on Hyperliquid, ANT is trading at $1.8T with $100M in daily trading volume and $200M in open interest (a big enough market to provide some signal). So perhaps traders are a bit more bullish on ANT price action than Wall Street.

Alongside the IPO sits a $26 billion private placement of non-voting Class N stock, bought at the IPO price by seven of Anthropic’s own compute suppliers. Google and Nvidia take about $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft about $1 billion apiece, TSMC $500 million. Those shares carry no lock-up. Combined with the IPO, Anthropic walks away with roughly $84.6 billion for general corporate purposes, mostly compute.

Regarding financials, Anthropic just reported revenue of $33.2 billion through nine months against $2.5 billion a year earlier and a $1.26 billion operating loss. It reported a $104 billion net loss, almost all of it a non-cash charge on convertible notes. And $900.9 billion in committed spending, with $70.7 billion due inside twelve months.

The liquidity drain thesis has been circulating since April, when CoinDesk laid out the case that mega-IPOs pull speculative money from the same pool that funds crypto. Retail allocations are the mechanism. A dollar earmarked for an IPO ticket is a dollar not in Bitcoin or memecoins.

We already ran this experiment. SpaceX listed June 12 at more than $2 trillion. Bitcoin fell 29% going into it, bottomed at $58,500 on June 30, and then rose 37% off that low. But notably, the Bitcoin bottom was effectively in on June 5 at $59k, a full week ahead of the SPCX IPO.

So the question is not whether an IPO pulls money out. It’s whether that already happened. Bitcoin fell roughly 15% from its early-October high through a $6.3 billion exchange hack, an AI capex selloff and a Democratic sweep.

Two things make this deal smaller than it looks for crypto. The retail slice is roughly $12.9 billion against SpaceX’s $22 billion, about 59% as much. And the $26 billion private placement never touches public market liquidity at all, because it comes from Anthropic’s vendors, who then get paid back in compute contracts.

Thus, if the SpaceX pattern repeats, and with the ~60% factor, we could expect BTC to drop another 5% or so into the Anthropic IPO (notably, that’s right where the 200-week moving average is). And we would expect the effective bottom to be in sometime in the next 1-2 days.

The good news is, the pain is mostly behind us, in that scenario. We will find out soon enough if IPO history repeats itself for crypto…

#4991. What Is Creatine HCl—and Is It Better Than Creatine Monohydrate?

CREATINE MONOHYDRATE HAS been seen as the gold standard in creatine use for years, so what’s up with creatine-HCl? This is a product that’s been showing up more on social media and supplement shelves.

People online have claimed that creatine-HCl helps you get swole quicker with greater performance benefits than the more commonly used creatine monohydrate. Considering that creatine-HCl products tend to cost more than creatine monohydrate, is it worth forking over the extra dough? Let’s investigate.

CREATINE-HCL STANDS FOR creatine hydrochloride. This is a salt form of creatine, explains Scott Keatley, RD, co-owner of Keatley Medical Nutrition Therapy. The main difference between hydrochloride and monohydrate is that creatine-HCl dissolves faster in water.

SOME PEOPLE ON TikTok claim that creatine-HCl is better because it’s absorbed more easily in the muscles and has no loading phase. While creatine-HCl dissolves more easily in water, Keatley says that doesn't automatically mean it leads to better results. The no-loading-phase claim is true, says Keatley, but monohydrate doesn’t require loading either.

“It's like the difference between a 99 percent and 100 percent,” says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab. “There's not a meaningful difference to you as a consumer.” Matheny argues that creatine-HCl's rise in popularity really comes from good, strategic marketing.

Shane Davis, MD, sports medicine physician and physiatrist at Tufts Medical Center, has dug into any scientific evidence that could justify creatine-HCl as a better alternative for building muscle. But he says there’s really nothing out there that points to creatine-HCl as a superior option to creatine monohydrate.

One study split 40 participants into groups to compare the effects of each form of creatine. After eight weeks, the researchers found that both forms “significantly enhanced” the effects of resistance training but showed “no benefit” of using creatine-HCl over creatine monohydrate.

OUR GUIDE TO THE BEST CREATINE SUPPLEMENTS

THE CLEAREST ADVANTAGE Keatley sees is that creatine-HCl is easier to mix into a drink than creatine monohydrate since it dissolves more readily. Because of that, proponents for creatine-HCl also market the supplement as more gut-friendly and usable in smaller doses.

While some people find that creatine-HCl is easier on the stomach, there isn't strong evidence that it consistently causes fewer digestive issues, Keatley says. “Claims that much smaller doses deliver the same benefits also haven’t been convincingly established,” he continues. “I’d describe better solubility as a real advantage, while the other claims run ahead of the evidence.”

Ultimately, when asked if creatine-HCl is worth the extra splurge, dietitians were unanimous in saying it’s just pure marketing fluff; it’s all creatine at the end of the day.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#4990. Bitmine posts $6.3B net loss for fiscal 2026 as Ether prices slide

Bitmine booked $6.2 billion in unrealized losses on digital assets. It held 6.07 million ETH on Sunday, or 4.97% of Ether’s supply.

Bitmine Immersion Technologies reported a $6.3 billion net loss for the fiscal year ended Aug. 31, driven largely by falling Ether (ETH) prices.

The company recorded a $6.2 billion unrealized loss on its digital assets, according to the Form 10-K it filed Tuesday. Bitmine posted a $348.6 million net profit the previous fiscal year.

Bitmine reported $15.4 billion in total assets and $33.7 million in liabilities at fiscal year-end. It held 5.9 million Ether worth $14.6 billion. Assets also included $545 million in cash and cash equivalents, 211 Bitcoin (BTC) valued at $16.6 million, $186 million in Beast Industries shares and $77 million in Eightco Holdings shares.

Bitmine’s revenue totaled $124.8 million, above the $119.8 million average analyst estimate listed by Yahoo Finance. Staking and validation accounted for $121.4 million, or 97% of revenue.

Bitmine shares closed 2.8% lower at $18.08 on Tuesday, bringing their year-to-date decline to 33%, according to Yahoo Finance. The company filed the 10-K after the market closed.

Bitmine, the largest corporate Ether holder, said in a Monday update that its holdings grew to 6.07 million ETH, or 4.97% of the supply. It estimated $309 million in annualized rewards on the 5.23 million ETH it had staked.

During a keynote at Token2049 in Singapore on Oct. 7, Bitmine Chairman Tom Lee called 5% of Ether’s supply a “hard cap” for the company’s holdings.

#4989. In This Midcentury Nashville Home a Mosaic Rainbow-Hued Window Brings Color to the Earthy Interiors

Here’s one you may not have heard before: What do Serge Mouille sconces, a Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant, and Borge Mogensen dining chairs have in common? Hint, hint: They all live in the Nashville home of Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of antiques and vintage furniture store Eneby Home.

One might reasonably presume the home of antiques purveyors to be chock-full of one-of-a-kind finds from far-flung locales, and while that is certainly the case here, the husband-and-wife duo’s house otherwise expertly subverts expectations.

Built in 1970, the uber-quiet four-acre property sits at the top of a hill overlooking seemingly endless rolling valleys. It’s surrounded by native honeysuckle bushes and Tennessee Fieldstone, and the sun sets on the front side of the home at 6:37 p.m., says Doug, who dreamed of building what he describes as an “art gallery” for their furniture.

The Jenkinses kept the wall of original Tennessee Fieldstone—and the original copper fireplace—but added their own flair with vintage furnishings, like a La Mura sofa, Gianfranco Frattini for Cassina leather lounge chairs, a Charles Dudouyt credenza, and a 1960s marble fossil coffee table from Italy. “It’s super unique,” says Doug. “It doesn’t have a name behind it.”

Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of Eneby Home, with their daughters. They kept the original Tennessee Fieldstone on the wall and paired it with an antique marble dining table that’s “huge and heavy,” says Doug. “The guys hated bringing stuff to our house.” Even though the Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant is often seen in copper, the Jenkins were able to source one with a green finish, seen here. The chairs are Borge Mogensen; the 1960s geometric painting is from France.

A well-lit workspace features a vintage 1950s desk, an Italian cocoon lamp, and a Hans Wegner chair from Denmark.

“There’s not a lot of this type of house in Nashville,” says Greg Dutton, the founder and architect behind Columbus, Ohio-based and AD PRO Directory design firm Dutton Studio. “But we had to look past what was there—a mixed bag with a new era in each room—to create a backdrop for the work they do.”

Cue the blank slate. The couple bought the house as-is—black toilets, mustard-hued siding, and early 2000s kitchen included—and embarked on a “bigger than expected” renovation, says Doug, that translated to moving walls, updating floor plans, a new roof and flooring, and overall furnishings, of course.

With the resulting minimalist white walls and clean lines, how does one amass the amount of character evident here? “When you build in materials that have inherent imperfection, that naturally bakes in a lot of warmth,” says Dutton. Think locally sourced, character-grade white oak flooring throughout; imported reclaimed European bluestone that’s well over 100 years old in the primary bath; and an original copper fireplace in the living room.

“The mosaic supposedly came from a building in Bologna,” says Doug. “The morning sun comes up on this side” and ignites a fire of filtered light within the home.

“The island has a commercial look to it,” says Doug of the Vipp system. “It hit every box. It’s sick.” Dutton helped reconfigure the space’s floor plan to open the room to the dining area. The oven is by Miele.

A Guillerme et Chambron cabinet, sourced from Eneby Home, houses curiosities. The 1960s pendant light is Stilnovo.

The guest bathroom originally had a black toilet and smoked glass. Now it features vintage Serge Mouille sconces, of which Doug has “never seen another pair,” a 250-year-old antique Italian mirror from the 1800s, and a Josef Hoffman bench.

“Flaws make the house feel better, not worse,” says Dutton. “But creating something that looks effortless actually takes a lot of coordination. We wanted it to feel like it had just happened that way, and installing materials that feel like they’ve been there forever automatically gives the house permanence and ensures it’ll age gracefully over time.”

The push-and-pull between old and new, simple and full of character, clean and distressed is one the Jenkins family is familiar with. The balancing act makes the house interesting, says Doug, and the same inclination to lean on historic materials translates just as well to furnishings.

“Some of this stuff we’d been saving in hopes we’d one day be able to use it for our own house,” says Doug. One such example is a 1940s or ’50s-era, rainbow-hued mosaic window the couple bought five years ago and stored in a crate ever since. “We asked Greg to change the layout of the wall to make it work,” says Doug. The stone sculpture on display in front of the window is another; it’s 2,000 years old, from Rome.

An earthy palette in the primary bedroom lets the landscape through the windows do the talking. The sconces are by Biny; the oversized 1880 landscape painting is from France; the chairs are Borge Mogensen; the side table is Charles Dudouyt, and the floor lamp is La Borne.

“The fireplace is original, and we ran with it,” says Doug. “It’s New Mexico style and is super cool.” It’s paired with an abstract geometric art piece and a collection of vases from Denmark.

The stainless steel Vipp island in the kitchen, which the couple found in Denmark, is “the best thing in the house,” says Carina. “We moved from a house with a big pantry, and it was a challenge to figure out where everything would go. This island holds a lot.” Here, the family embraces the “European model” of compact living–albeit in 4,500 square feet. Dutton designed the black cabinetry wall as a backdrop for the island, which he calls a “piece of jewelry.”

Like any gallerist worth his salt, Doug spends five minutes each night turning off all of the ambient lighting in the house, like a pair of midcentury Biny sconces in the primary bedroom, an Orocolo lamp in the living room, and a 1970s Marika Carniti Bollea Nautilus lamp in the kitchen. Much of the light shines on a collection of artwork that’s so vast it’s often swapped out.

“We’ve already traded a few things,” says Doug. “If we find something we love, we want to use it. It’s what we do. It’s a perk, for sure.”

As is a home that’s forever. “Our girls want to come here when we’re old with their own kids,” says Carina. “They love it so much.”

The 1950s painting is from Denmark, the 1970s vase is from Sweden, the 1970s stool is Pierre Chapo, and the ceramic is from France. “We spend a lot of time in Denmark and like to mix Danish and Swedish,” says Doug. “We know a lot about that style of furniture.”

Dutton reworked the floor plan in the primary bathroom to set the vanity under a skylight. “We don’t use much overhead lighting,” says Carina.
