#4986. Bitget Wallet reports sevenfold jump in crypto phishing risk alerts

The wallet flagged about 37,000 signing requests a week for phishing in Q3 and said better monitoring likely contributed to the increase.

Crypto wallet provider Bitget Wallet said Wednesday it was expanding AI-assisted threat detection after its weekly average of signing requests flagged for potential phishing rose 7.4-fold in the third quarter of 2026.

The wallet flagged about 37,000 such requests per week in Q3, up from 5,000 in Q2, according to Bitget Wallet chief operating officer Alvin Kan.

“The increase likely reflects both greater threat activity and improved detection capabilities, although the data does not allow us to quantify each factor’s contribution,” Kan told Cointelegraph.

The wallet introduced AI-assisted risk detection in Q2, he added.

Kan said that the tally counted flagged signing interactions, not unique attacks or confirmed financial losses.

Bitget Wallet said its expanded AI checks examine website code and domain registration details for signs of fraud. The wallet also simulates transactions to spot hidden permissions before users sign.

Bitget Wallet also said AI helped identify 90% of newly blocked risky domains in October, compared with 36% in Q2. Nearly 70% of domains its AI flagged that month had no matching alerts in the external threat feeds it checked.

The findings come amid continued phishing threats targeting crypto users. In July, a trader lost nearly $1 million after signing a malicious Ethereum token approval.

An August investigation by Rapid7 uncovered a phishing campaign that targeted 885,000 phone numbers and directed recipients to websites impersonating crypto wallet providers.

#4985. Decrypt Becomes First News Publisher to Offer Perps Trading, Powered by Solana's Phoenix

Decrypt Becomes First News Publisher to Offer Perps Trading, Powered by Solana's Phoenix

What happens when the place you read the news becomes the place you act on it?

The news that moves markets and the tools to trade them have long lived in separate places. Decrypt is bringing them together.

Decrypt is integrating perpetual futures trading, powered by Phoenix, a fully onchain perpetuals exchange on Solana, making it the first media platform to offer perps trading directly alongside its journalism.

Readers will be able to take long or short positions on crypto, stocks and commodities from the same place they follow the news that moves them, without switching apps, connecting external wallets or moving funds between platforms.

"Information influences decisions, shapes opinions and moves markets," said Ilan Hazan, co-founder and COO of Decrypt. "A reader might discover an opportunity through a publication, develop a view and then leave to act on it somewhere else. The publisher monetizes the impression, while someone else monetizes the transaction. It means the publisher captures only a small part of the economic activity that its information and distribution generates."

Rather than treating media and financial products as separate businesses, he said, Decrypt is bringing them together. "We're allowing readers to go from understanding what's happening in the markets to taking a position, without leaving Decrypt.”

"Partnering with Decrypt closes the gap between reading the news and acting on it," said Kabir Sethi, Head of Business Development at Phoenix. "With over 100 Phoenix perpetuals markets built directly into Decrypt, readers can take in a story, form a view, and trade it in the same place. We are excited to help play a part in transforming Decrypt into a true information exchange."

Perpetual futures, or perps, are contracts that let traders bet on an asset's price rising or falling without an expiry date, typically using leverage. Leverage magnifies both gains and losses, and positions can be liquidated quickly when markets move against them.

Phoenix offers markets in crypto, U.S. stocks, gold and pre-IPO companies around the clock, with up to 40x leverage and low fees, according to its website. It is developed by Ellipsis Labs, a team that has built exclusively on Solana and whose CEO, Eugene Chen, has described perps as having "crazy product market fit."

"These are very, very strong, high revenue businesses, probably some of the best in the industry," Chen said of perps exchanges in a podcast interview with Blockworks' Lightspeed earlier this year. Phoenix routes each trade at the moment it lands onchain, splitting it between its order book and market makers "based on what's going to give you the best price," he explained.

Perps trading runs through the Decrypt Money Account, a single self-custodial account on Solana designed to work without seed phrases, gas fees or bridging.

One balance now funds it all: prediction markets on Decrypt Predict, Jupiter-powered token swaps, Earn and, now, perpetual futures. Users can also send funds privately, after Decrypt added confidential transfers through Solana infrastructure firm Helius.

Imagine reading about a public company's earnings and taking a position on its stock, at any hour. Or following a breaking story about Bitcoin and going long or short in the same moment.

From information to execution, in one place.

Announced in September, The Information Exchange is Decrypt's vision for the next generation of financial media: a single destination bringing together news, intelligence, market data, probabilities, direct financial participation—with perps forming the latest building block in that infrastructure.

News moves markets. Now readers can move with it.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region.

#4984. Victorian Architecture Meets Mediterranean Charm in This Warm and Whimsical London Home

Interior designer Marta de la Rica describes herself as “a New Yorker by birth, French by adoption, and Spanish at heart.” That mix of cultures would prove to be an asset for one of her recent projects: the 5,382-square-foot four-story home of a family of four in London. A fine example of Victorian architecture, the building, located in the quiet residential neighborhood of Battersea and only steps away from Battersea Park, was built in 1879. “I already knew the clients, since I had previously designed their home in Bilbao, and I knew what kind of atmosphere the family wanted as well as what was particularly important to them about their home,” says de la Rica.

The walls of the stairwell are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks and the Clam Shell Large light fixtures are by Porta Romana. The floor is made of several different marbles: Negro Marquina, Perlado, and Rojo Alicante.

The young family is originally from Spain but had already been living in London for nearly 10 years when they decided to buy a single-family home there. De la Rica decided to design spaces for them with a British-Mediterranean look that reflects the character of the historic Victorian architecture and the demands of modern family life. “The family wanted an elegant and peaceful home that, above all, would be warm and cozy,” says de la Rica. “For us, that meant creating spaces with comfort, character, and personality.”

Two midcentury armchairs sit in front of a Louis XIV fireplace from 1700.

Light brown and rust tones dominate the living room’s color palette.

Since the clients are Spanish, the project had a particularly interesting cultural dimension for the designer, as she explains: “We wanted the house to feel personal to them while also remaining true to its London identity and the Victorian architecture. So the project became a dialogue between English interior design and a more relaxed, Mediterranean lifestyle,” says de la Rica.

De la Rica’s interiors combine English interior design and a relaxed lifestyle in this Victorian building where the sunroom doubles as a dining room.

Originally, the family had been looking for a home in a different part of London, but when they saw this house, they were immediately taken with it and changed their plans. The building’s structure was in very good condition; only the floor plan needed to be reconfigured. “Above all, we wanted to preserve the spacious rooms, the ceiling heights, the bay windows, and the staircase,” explains the designer.

A relaxed breakfast setting with chairs by Vintola Studio and Benjamin pendant lights from Felix Lighting,

Visually, all the rooms have an almost opulent feel, yet at the same time, each one reflects its particular purpose. The ground floor includes a study, a living room, a kitchen, af amily room, and a sunroom, which also serves as the dining room. The parents’ bedroom is located on the second floor. A separate children’s area with bedrooms and play areas was created on the top floor. “The sunroom is a big part of the character of the house. To build the basement, it had to be demolished and rebuilt. It was very important to us to preserve the extraordinary presence of this space and its connection to the rest of the house,” says de la Rica.

The designer made the transitions from one room to the next as natural as possible by ensuring that the materials and atmosphere flow seamlessly.

The living spaces flow into the garden (designed by Matthew Wilson) and the terraces in front of the family room and the sunroom provide direct access to the outdoors. The garden features a pool, an outdoor kitchen, and a spacious dining area, providing additional spaces for social gatherings. The interior is characterized by elegant craftsmanship, custom details, warm colors, and high-quality materials. These choices highlight the grandeur of the historic home while also creating a cozy atmosphere.

A 20th-century dresser serves as a nightstand in the primary bedroom; an onyx lamp sits next to a vase filled with flowers.

“I particularly like that the rooms all have their own character, yet there is a clear, unifying design that carries throughout the house,” says the designer. It was also important to de la Rica to emphasize the scale of the house without having it feel cold or ostentatious. “Even in the larger rooms, there’s a coziness. It was very important to us to achieve this balance,” she says.

In the guest bathroom, the walls are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks. Below the Negro Marquina marble sink by MDLR-Design, a curtain of Marly fabric by Colony.

The playroom showcases the Victorian architecture.

The children’s rooms are located under the mansard roof, which is typical of many Victorian houses in London. For the two youngest members of the family, the designer transformed the entire top floor with its sloped ceilings into colorful playrooms.

Amid dinosaurs and Mickey Mouse toys, the family’s son sleeps here. A Globe ceiling light hangs above the bed (Olofane).

De la Rica’s favorite parts of the house, however, are the living room and the dining room. For her, these are the areas where the home truly comes to life and where the design directly interacts with the family’s daily routine.

Above the large sofa in the family room, a Soren globe light from Pinch. The cream-colored Mayan Cuzco rug is from Bellbridge.

She particularly likes that the living room, dining room, and kitchen form a single, open space. “This part of the house allows for a very uncomplicated way of living,” she explains. “It’s very beautiful but not contrived or overly formal. It exudes coziness and spontaneity, and for me, that’s ultimately what makes a family home.”

For the walls in this bathroom, the designer chose Oli-03 Smooth by Clayworks, wall sconces by Vaughan, and a Calacatta Viola marble sink.

The wardrobe in the guest bedroom is upholstered with Ticking Stripe 1 Black fabric (Ian Mankin). The decorative Flax 03 tassels are from Samuel & Sons.

In the daughter’s bedroom, the rug is from Kvadrat. The closet doors are upholstered with fabrics from Yutes.

The Bert & May tiles in Garnet and Sand White were custom-made for this bathroom. The bathtub is Perlado marble.

This article originally appeared in the German edition of Architectural Digest.

#4983. Bitcoin short-term holders send 47K BTC to exchanges at a loss

Bitcoin fell to an 11-week low of $72,670 after slipping below short-term holders’ average cost basis of about $75,700, according to CryptoQuant.

Bitcoin (BTC) short-term holders sent more than 47,000 BTC to exchanges on Tuesday as Bitcoin fell to an 11-week low, with the coins worth less than their purchase price.

Data from onchain analytics platform CryptoQuant showed short-term holders sent 47,025 BTC carrying unrealized losses to exchanges in the 24 hours to midnight UTC at the start of Wednesday.

The tally was among the largest 24-hour totals in the four months of data tracked by CryptoQuant and came as Bitcoin dropped to $72,670, its lowest level since Aug. 20.

The short-term holder metric tracks Bitcoin held for up to 155 days. Short-term holders are generally considered more likely to sell when prices fall, even at a loss.

“On November 10, profitable transfers stood near 1,700 BTC, meaning approximately 27 BTC arrived at a loss for every 1 BTC transferred at a profit,” CryptoQuant said.

The largest 24-hour inflows of BTC carrying unrealized losses since late July occurred on Oct. 8, hours after Bitcoin fell back to $80,000. Rolling 24-hour inflows peaked at 50,500 BTC.

Short-term holders’ exchange inflows of BTC carrying unrealized losses. Source: CryptoQuant

Cointelegraph previously reported that short-term holders’ average cost basis was around $75,700.

Related: Stock ETF buyers pounce on dip as Bitcoin funds bleed $1.6 billion

Short-term holders moved into an aggregate unrealized profit on Aug. 20 as Bitcoin rebounded toward $80,000. They slipped back into an aggregate unrealized loss on Friday.

STH cost basis vs. BTC/USD. Source: CryptoQuant

CryptoQuant head of research Julio Moreno said Bitcoin could still be in the downtrend that began in November 2025.

“If rallies keep stalling near the cohort’s cost basis and net unrealized losses deepen, the August rebound starts to look like a pause in the broader downtrend,” he wrote on Monday.

STH-NUPL vs. BTC/USD. Source: CryptoQuant

CryptoQuant’s net unrealized profit/loss (NUPL) metric showed that short-term holders reached their highest level of unrealized profitability since November 2025 as Bitcoin climbed to $87,200 in September.

#4980. OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue Sets Launch, Turning to Crypto Companies After Big Tech Opts Out

OKX-ICE Tokenized Stock Venue Sets Launch, Turning to Crypto Companies After Big Tech Opts Out

After weeks of rejections from corporate America, OKX and ICE’s tokenized stock venue has found its launch lineup: crypto’s own public companies.

OKXICE said Wednesday that its Tokenized Securities Venue will open Nov. 23, letting eligible U.S. customers trade tokenized securities around the clock in the OKX app.

More than 70 assets are expected to be listed, including securities from Coinbase, Circle, Robinhood, Strategy, BitGo, Securitize and Better.com, plus 59 ETFs from Direxion. Strategy will also list its preferred stocks.

That’s a very different roster from the one the venue started with. As of its Nov. 5 notice, 67 companies, including every member of the “Magnificent Seven” tech giants, had objected to having their shares traded on the platform. Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy were notably absent from that list, and all three are now among the venue’s launch issuers.

“The SEC has created a real opening for American markets to lead the next era of finance,” said former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, who co-chairs OKXICE, a joint venture between OKX and NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange.

Securitize will provide the tokenization infrastructure. Each token represents an entitlement backed one-for-one by a real U.S. share, carrying dividends, voting rights and other shareholder benefits, according to the announcement.

Trading will run through automated market maker pools built on Uniswap v4 on X Layer, OKX’s Ethereum layer-2 network, and customers will hold their tokens in self-custodial wallets.

Strategy CEO Phong Le called tokenization on the venue “a natural next step” for the company’s preferred stocks, such as STRC.

Access is limited to eligible U.S.-based OKX customers who activate a CeDeFi Wallet, pass identity verification and complete onboarding with OKXICE. The venue operates under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate. Under the exemption, companies can keep third-party tokenized versions of their stock off a given venue by objecting within 30 days.

The launch caps a busy stretch for Wall Street tokenization. DTCC’s new tokenization service, which runs partly on the Canton Network, went live in late October, and Morgan Stanley said it was among the first banks to use it.

#4979. Kraken parent adds over 200 tokenized UK and EU stocks to xStocks

The expansion adds more than 200 tokenized UK and EU equities, including all 40 companies in Germany’s DAX index, as xStocks plans further expansion into Hong Kong.

Kraken parent Payward’s xStocks platform added tokenized versions of more than 200 stocks listed in the United Kingdom and European Union.

Payward said Wednesday that eligible users can trade the new tokens on Kraken and participating xStocks Alliance platforms. The tokens remain unavailable to investors in the UK.

The expansion includes tokenized shares of all 40 companies in Germany’s benchmark DAX index, including SAP, Siemens, Allianz and Deutsche Telekom. According to a separate Wednesday announcement from Deutsche Börse, the tokens are available for trading on its affiliated 360X platform, with 24/7 trading supported on select venues.

Payward said the latest expansion brings xStocks’ offering to more than 1,500 tokenized assets. The company reported more than $45 billion in cumulative transaction volume and over 550,000 unique holders.

According to Payward, the new tokens are backed one-to-one by shares held with a regulated custodian. Kraken’s risk disclosure says xStocks tokens give holders exposure to the shares’ performance without voting rights or legal claims to the underlying stock.

Token Terminal data put xStocks’ market capitalization at $1.1 billion, about 22% of the $4.9 billion in tokenized stocks the firm tracks. xStocks ranked third, behind Binance bStocks at $1.5 billion and Ondo Finance at $1.4 billion.

Payward said it plans to add hundreds of Hong Kong-listed equities in the coming weeks.

#4978. 7 Best Work Totes, Tested by Style Editors

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Finding the right work tote is surprisingly tough. It needs to be durable, secure, organized, comfortable, and, hopefully, stylish too. If you’re like our editors, you might be stuffing everything from water bottles to emergency umbrellas in your everyday bag. A good work tote may even be able to double as a carry-on or weekender bag.

While I work remotely, I’m often toting my work setup with me to my favorite coffee shop, coworking space, or a friend’s house to get work done. So, I know a thing or two about what matters when you’re looking for a durable bag that can securely hold all your everyday essentials (and more) for years without signs of wear and tear.

Work totes are some of the most functional bags you can own, too. The best work totes often reduce strain on your back and shoulders, since their handles make them easy to carry. They can also allow you to access the items you need most quickly. For example, most bags are designed with smaller pockets so you can easily store and grab your phone or wallet in a jiffy.

Not all work totes are created equal, though. A lack of functional pockets can make you feel disorganized, and straps that dig into your shoulders can make your commute miserable. Our Women’s Health editors tested more than 20 work totes and spoke to fashion experts to the find the best options for every budget.

From work to travel, the Apryl Laptop Tote Bag is the best overall work tote for its smart compartment placement, durability, and comfy straps. Even better: At $50, it’s budget-friendly, too. When our tester first ordered this bag, they didn’t have high expectations for it because of the affordable price point, but it quickly became the best tote they tested.

Our tester is obsessed with the bag’s comfort, which makes it easy to hold and tote. Made of faux leather, the bag has structured but flexible straps that don’t dig into your shoulders or hands, no matter how long you hold the bag. Inside the bag, you’ll find a padded laptop pocket that closes with Velcro to keep your device secure. There are also five additional smaller compartments inside (all cotton-lined), including a water bottle holder that’s also padded.

One thing I particularly love about this bag is that it’s also designed with two exterior compartments, one on each side of the bag, that each close with magnetic buttons. "I found these useful for throwing keys, and other items I don’t necessarily need throughout the day, like an emergency Tylenol," our tester says.

When you put the bag down, it lies flat, which makes it really easy to reach inside and find specific items. However, the bag doesn’t have studs at the bottom, so it does touch whatever you place it on. While the red color our tester received can hide stains or dirt fairly well, another color option, like the beige choice, may not.

I tested Quince’s Leather Shopper Tote, and it has quickly become my go-to work bag. While most bags on this list have a laptop pocket, this work tote has the best one. Its laptop compartment is lined in soft suede, which protects your device against scratches. I also like to throw my sunglasses in there, since I’m pretty bad about carrying their case, but I’m always afraid the keys in my bag will rough them up. What I love about this zippered laptop holder, which sits in the center of the purse, is that it also serves as the bag’s divider, essentially dividing the bag into two separate compartments. It’s almost like you have two purses.

I like using one side to store my work essentials, like my planner or other important documents, and the other for extras, like my makeup, hand cream, and, on rarer occasions, even a pair of shoes. Each side also has smaller compartments to increase your organization options: One side has two smaller compartments, while the other has just one. I do wish at least one of these compartments was zippered to keep small items, like my wallet, more secure during crowded commutes, but I just throw it in the same pocket as my laptop, and that works for me.

I also like that this bag is structured and more on the rigid side because it gives the bag a just-purchased feel, while giving me a more put-together look. I find that the straps are a little too thin, though, which, as a chronic overpacker, makes me worry about how much of a load the bag can really handle. I’ve had experiences where I’ve hung bags with thin straps onto hooks (like those inside a bathroom stall) only to have a strap detach from the bag, and it’s not the best experience. So while I do use some work totes for short weekend trips, this is one I would likely leave behind.

Still, it’s the perfect bag for professional settings or errand-running on weekends. Its price point of $150 is also exceptional for a bag made from real leather. Other options, like the Cuyana bag in this guide, are much more pricey.

Amazon has a boatload of bags that look pricier than they are, and, at around $35, this work tote is just that. It’s designed with a crocodile pattern that gives the bag a stylish touch and high-end feel, which is what originally drew me to it, but it’s also one of the best-rated tote bags on the site. Like our best overall bag, it’s made with a padded laptop pocket that closes with a small Velcro strap, but there’s also a zipper compartment in the middle that’s big enough to store a laptop.

Inside the bag, you’ll find two small mesh pockets on the narrower sides, perfect for storing small valuables, pens, or travel makeup essentials. “On days I wore a watch to the office, I would take it off and store it in one of those pockets until it was time to leave (I find it hard to type and wear a link-chain watch at the same time because of the friction between my hand and the desk),” one tester says. “I also used those pockets to store cough drops, lip gloss, and other trinkets I didn’t want to get lost in the abyss of my work tote.”

There’s a smaller zippered pocket inside of the purse, which I appreciate for storing items you want to keep secure, like cash, plus two other smaller compartments for a phone, wallet, or other mid-sized items. For perspective, you could probably fit a bottle of perfume in each of them.

I also appreciate this bag’s versatility. It’s designed like an accordion, meaning it widens when stuffed to the brim, but appears narrower when it’s not packing a heavy load. The purse also comes with an additional strap, so you can detach the tote’s carrying handles and use it as a crossbody bag if that’s your preference.

Our tester’s only concern with this bag is that the gold accents on the straps may fade over time, which may make the bag unsightly. However, they have used it for over a month, including on rainy days, and have had no issues yet.

There’s just something about real leather that feels so luxe. That’s one of the reasons this leather tote from Cuyana is worth the splurge. The material feels soft and has proven to be durable, despite how much our tester stuffed this bag or threw it on the ground after a long day.

If you prefer a little more organization, you can buy accessories that attach to the inside of the bag using button clasps. They particularly liked that the straps are built into the bag, instead of sewn onto it. “This gives me extra reassurance that this purse won’t fall apart on me when I’m on the go,” they say. “The straps are also pretty wide and flexible, so they feel very comfy on my shoulders, no matter how heavy the bag is.” It is a little tougher to wear this tote on your shoulder when wearing a thick, puffy coat, but it just takes a little more effort to throw on than some of the other bags on this list.

The Longchamp Le Pliage Tote is a cult-classic bag that you’ve probably noticed someone else carrying on your commute before. The large nylon tote with leather trim comes from a luxury French brand, which explains the higher price tag.

While it doesn’t offer a lot of internal organization—just two pockets—the large internal compartment is great for those who need to stash bulkier items, like an extra pair of shoes.

Plus, the water-resistant nylon exterior is flexible and durable, so you don’t have to worry about the elements as much as with an all-leather bag.

With the Le Pliage Tote, you’re paying for the Longchamp name and classic design, but you could definitely do worse than a bag as durable and functional as this one.

From one of our favorite luggage brands, the Monos Metro Tote is perfect for someone who travels frequently for work or fun. The vegan leather design is simple, yet stylish, and it comes in six colors to coordinate with your luggage set.

While it’s priced at nearly $250, the bag boasts the most extensive organization I’ve seen in a work tote, including a laptop compartment, water bottle holder, and handy exterior pocket for quickly stashing items during airport security. And the zipper closure to keep your belongings secure, whether commuting or jet-setting.

You may know Baggu for its reusable grocery totes, but the brand also makes durable purses and work totes in its signature slouchy, patterned nylon style. This shoulder bag is for someone who packs light and just needs a versatile, casual bag to carry a laptop and a few extra items.

The Baggu Shoulder Bag is a workhorse with a thick, adjustable strap, zipper closure, and a spot to attach your keys. The tote is completely machine washable, which is an anomaly among work bags. However, the bag’s main compartment is narrow with space for a 14-inch laptop and a slim notebook or two.

At around $50, it’s a great everyday bag and comes in a fun variety of colors and patterns, including leopard and gingham, for someone who wants to show a little personality at work.

While work bags are functional, we also want them to be stylish and versatile, so you don’t have to switch bags for a different outfit. Most of our favorite work totes are simple, timeless silhouettes with a few exceptions, like the Baggu Shoulder Bag for a slouchy look.

The material of your bag will often come down to style preference, but some materials are more functional than others. For example, canvas bags are durable, lightweight, and great for everyday use. That said, they do take a little more effort to clean when soiled, since canvas doesn’t dry quickly. You may need to give it some time to air out after spot cleaning.

Another option: Leather totes—these are chic and long-lasting. Just keep in mind that they can be pricier than other totes because they’re investment pieces that should last for years. They’re also versatile and stylish enough to take to post-work activities. (Read: You won’t look weird showing up to happy hour with this as your plus-one.)

Vegan leather is also a great option because it’s a more affordable and cruelty-free option, and some of them look pretty realistic. Just keep in mind that vegan leathers can be made from a variety of materials, and some are more durable than others. For example, some vegan leathers made from low-quality plastic are prone to peeling.

Finally, nylon or polyester bags are perfect for a more casual, sporty vibe. They are easy to clean and can really take a roughing up with day-to-day use.

Look for a bag with both larger and smaller compartments, which will make organizing and protecting your important items so much easier, says fashion contributor Rose Lauture. For example, the zippered laptop compartment in Quince’s Italian leather tote is lined with suede to avoid scratching it. Meanwhile, smaller compartments will prevent you from being forced to dig through all the random stuff that accumulates in a work tote to find your phone, pen, or lip gloss.

Think about how you use your tote bag when searching for the right size and organizational capabilities. For example, if you like to pack lunch or bring your gym clothes along, then you’ll need a bag with a wide open compartment for bulkier items. If your main goal is to stay organized and pack light, then a smaller bag with interior pockets may help you on the go.

The closure of the bag will depend on your needs. Totes with a zip or snap closure are great for securing your items and protecting them against thieves, Lauture says. It also ensures that your belongings won’t be scattered across the floor if the bag falls over. But if you prefer accessibility instead of security, open totes may be a more convenient option, she adds.

Having a work tote with comfortable straps can make or break your commute, whether you catch a train into the city or walk to and from your car. Look for bags with thick, durable straps that are tall enough so the bag doesn’t rub against your armpit. Some work totes even come with a crossbody option or adjustable strap, if you prefer to tailor the bag to your height.

I’m always working on the go, so I have a lot of experience carrying around work totes, from faux leather to canvas options. I did extensive research on work totes from top brands to come up with an initial list for this guide, choosing a variety of sizes, materials, and price points. I got hands-on with the runner-up tote from Quince, and one Women’s Health tester with more than 12 years of experience using work totes spent a month testing the other bags.

From commuting to weekend trips, I evaluated the work totes on factors like design, material, compartments, comfort, and more.

#4977. Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.

#4976. Barclays boosts BlackRock Bitcoin ETF stake 48% as banks expand holdings

Barclays reported a $187 million stake as of Sept. 30, while Citigroup disclosed a new $119 million position in Fidelity’s Bitcoin ETF.

Barclays reported holding 3.94 million shares in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter, up 48% from the previous quarter.

The position in the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) was worth about $187 million on Sept. 30, up from $88.5 million for 2.66 million shares on June 30, according to Barclays’ Friday Form 13F filing.

“Early readings suggest longer-term holders make up a growing share of the spot bitcoin ETF investor base,” Bitwise analyst Camran Khosravi told Cointelegraph, pointing to increased bank exposure, including at Citigroup.

Citigroup reported a new position worth about $119 million in the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC). Its reported IBIT holdings also rose 50% to about 521,000 shares, from 347,000 at the end of June.

The filings offer an early indication of changing institutional Bitcoin ETF ownership, with more major banks and hedge funds yet to disclose their third-quarter positions. Institutional investment managers have until Monday to file their third-quarter holdings reports.

Banks accounted for $258 million, or 4.9%, of reported spot Bitcoin ETF holdings among early third-quarter filers, up from $107 million, or 3.2%, in Q2, according to Bitwise data shared with Cointelegraph.

Investment advisers remained the largest holders, accounting for $3.5 billion, or 66.6%, of reported positions, up from $2.2 billion in the previous quarter. Bitwise compared holdings across the two quarters for the 1,389 institutions that had filed their third-quarter reports by Friday. The value of their combined spot Bitcoin ETF holdings rose to $5.25 billion from $3.35 billion.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded products (ETP) holdings of early Q3 2026 13F filers. Source: Bitwise

Khosravi said financial advisers at major banks and wealth platforms can now recommend Bitcoin ETFs to clients, a development he expects to become increasingly visible in institutional filings over the coming quarters.

Related: Global banks commit $270M to stablecoin venture targeting 2027 launch

The figures remain preliminary, with major holders including JPMorgan, Wells Fargo and hedge funds Millennium and Schonfeld yet to report their positions ahead of the upcoming deadline.

Rising Bitcoin ETF valuations do not necessarily reflect fresh institutional buying, according to Jonatan Randin, senior market analyst at PrimeXBT.

“It’s important to look at the holdings in shares and not just the increase in dollar amount,” Randin told Cointelegraph, noting that IBIT gained about 41% in price in Q3.

Separately, an October CoinShares survey found that diversification accounted for 40% of responses on why investors held digital assets, compared with less than 9% for speculation.

Randin cautioned that the filings capture holdings as of Sept. 30, before Bitcoin’s subsequent decline and outflows from spot ETFs. US spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded about $6.34 billion in net inflows during the third quarter, followed by roughly $2.1 billion in net outflows in Q4 through Tuesday, according to SoSoValue data.

#4975. A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

A High-Severity Bug Could Have Stalled Bitcoin Mining—Bitcoin Core Has a Fix

Bitcoin Core developers have disclosed three security vulnerabilities in the software that runs most of the Bitcoin network, including one high-severity bug that could disrupt mining, all of which have since been fixed.

Bitcoin Core, the reference software operating the majority of the network’s nodes, classifies vulnerabilities into four tiers, from “low” to “critical,” and these fell into the middle of that range.

The most serious, tracked as CVE-2025-62861, concerns block template creation, the process miners rely on to assemble new blocks.

According to the advisory, a chain reorganization could trigger an erroneous eviction of certain transactions, corrupting a node’s mempool, its waiting room for unconfirmed transactions, so that an affected node could no longer build block templates until it was restarted. For a miner, that means a halt to the very work that earns block rewards.

A second flaw, CVE-2025-62859, could let a peer stall a node’s block processing by announcing a compact block with a manipulated witness, a form of transaction malleation.

None of the three rises to the “critical” level Bitcoin Core reserves for bugs that threaten coin theft at the protocol level, unauthorized coin creation or permanent network-wide chain splits. The developers did not report any evidence the flaws were exploited.

The disclosures come amid a broader reckoning over how quickly Bitcoin’s defenders can keep pace with a shifting threat landscape.

Bitcoin Core recently rewrote its security policy to account for AI, adding a category for “easily discoverable” vulnerabilities that can be surfaced through a simple AI prompt, which it now patches and discloses immediately rather than holding under a long embargo.

That shift reflects mounting concern that large language models have lowered the bar for finding flaws, a worry underscored by the volunteer “Bitcoin Red Team,” which has been racing to uncover AI-discoverable bugs across the ecosystem before attackers can exploit them.

The fixes themselves shipped earlier, with the advisories following under Bitcoin Core’s disclosure timeline. Node operators are generally urged to run the latest software to stay protected.

#4973. I Spent Years Hiding My Ulcerative Colitis. One Night at Sundance Changed How I Saw My Disease

In an exclusive excerpt from her new book, NBC correspondent Chloe Melas shares the reality of navigating an early relationship with IBD.

For 15 years, I've battled a form of Inflammatory Bowel Disease (IBD) known as Ulcerative Colitis. It's an autoimmune disease in which your body attacks your colon. When I was diagnosed at 25-years-old after finding blood in my stool, I felt like I had nowhere to turn because neither side of my family has gut issues. I thought if maybe I ignored my symptoms (the blood, the bloating, the constipation), it would eventually go away. It didn't. As I continued to chase my career and search for love, things got worse and I eventually couldn't hide it anymore. When I went to the Sundance Film Festival I was at the height of my disease and I felt like it was following me like a cloud trying to rain on every moment of joy. What follows is a story—from my new book that I co-authored with Judy Batalion, The Girl’s Guide to Guts—that I never planned to share publicly. But I am now in hopes of helping anyone out there feel less alone. — Chloe Melas

As I rose up through the TV ranks, my invitations became better and better. I wasn’t at the end of the red carpets anymore; I was now just a few spots from the end! One of the best parts of the job is being able to attend some of the most exclusive events, like the Golden Globe Awards, private concerts, and the annual Sundance Film Festival, which, until 2026, was held at the foot of the Rocky Mountains in Park City, Utah. Sundance, started by the late actor Robert Redford, is a week-long party celebrating the best in film from that year, with a who’s who of Hollywood descending on this snowy, storybook town.

In 2012, worlds collided when (my now-husband) Brian and I first started dating, because he coincidentally hosted the hardest-to-get-into party at Sundance. Just a few months into dating, he invited me to come along. I said yes before even checking to see if I had enough vacation days. Somehow I convinced my editor that I should go and cover all the celebrity happenings. I also told her it would cost the company nothing because I had been invited by a film studio, which was a total lie—I put it all on my credit card and told myself I’d figure it out later. Luckily, she said yes!

Brian and I had already been on a few trips, but it was getting harder to pretend I was headed to the gym for a workout when I really was going to find a lobby loo. Eventually I had to come clean, because saying I was going to hit the gym at 11 p.m. after a couple of cocktails wasn’t passing his BS meter.

But the good news was, my flares were intermittent. And because I had a really good poker face, he had no clue just how bad things could get. Plus, over the eighteen months since my symptoms first wreaked havoc on my life, I’d become a pro on how to mentally map out bathrooms wherever we went. I’d also order sparingly at dinner. I didn’t want to be holed up in some bathroom while he was out there wondering what he’d gotten himself into. Trying to hide my disease while also managing my day-to-day emotional spirals, however, became a lot to handle.

We finally made it to Park City, and from our balcony we could see the snow-covered mountains in the distance and the beautifully dressed celebrities in their winter wear dotting the streets below us. I couldn’t wait to wear an outfit I had been saving in my suitcase—the all-white look. We’re talking a faux fur vest and matching beanie, topped off with the cutest low-rise skinny jeans. I looked like an influencer before there even were influencers, y’all!

As Brian and I made our way to a rooftop party, I suddenly felt the urge. My eyes immediately darted to a hallway on the other side of these massive sliding glass doors. It was a single-stall bathroom and the line was too long. I had to make a move, and fast. I tugged on Brian’s sweater and whispered, “I need to run back to the hotel.” He understood it was urgent code for needing to use the bathroom. He handed me our room key, and I couldn’t have left any faster.

As I started speed-walking toward our hotel, the soft buzz of the alcohol slowly began to dissipate, and my intermittent depression began to creep in. Here we were, in this beautiful city at a dreamlike event, and it had been interrupted once again by my disease. Can’t I catch a break? I thought.

As I crossed the street to our hotel, the need to go was only intensifying. Almost there, I said to myself. Squeeze your cheeks!

I got to the elevator bank and pushed the up button several times, as if that would make the elevator move faster. I painfully watched the numbers click at a glacial pace from floor to floor: eight . . . seven . . . six . . . And then, like a volcano about to erupt, my poor body just couldn’t keep it in. I prayed it would just be some flatulence.

Luckily, no one was around, but I was devastated, embarrassed, and defeated. When I got back to the hotel room I threw away those new, gorgeous jeans that now represented something so painful. I sat and cried.

“How much longer can I live like this?” I whimpered.

I didn’t feel like going back out. I had worked too hard to prevent ulcerative colitis from stealing my joy and taking over my life. That day, I had lost the battle. I had assumed that if I could map bathrooms in every space I entered, limit what I ate, and take my medicine, situations like this wouldn’t happen. So much of ulcerative colitis was accepting that most of this was totally out of my control. I texted Brian that I was going to skip the rest of the events that afternoon. He came back and just held me on the bed.

This was the only time I ever soiled myself. But I can’t tell you the countless times I’ve busted into coffee shops and restaurants to beg for the code to the bathroom only to be told I’d need to wait in line and buy something.

They say you’re supposed to know your nearest exit. But as someone living with bowel disease, I’ve always scouted the nearest bathroom. If I was going to be traveling, I’d time my meals so that I could avoid having to make a pit stop on long car rides, train trips, or flights. I’d even get dehydrated, because I’d be so nervous that even water would trigger me. I was withering away both physically and emotionally.

It sounds dramatic, and that’s because it is. But I have managed to find the humor. I can even laugh (a little) now at what happened at Sundance and say to myself, Well, at least Brad Pitt wasn’t standing in the elevator next to me.

#4971. 9 Best Desks for Small Spaces That Offer Savvy Organization (2026)

A desk in a small apartment rarely gets a room of its own. More often it shares a wall with the bed, borrows a corner of the living room, or has to hide somewhere when guests come over. This means the best desks for small spaces really earn their footprint. We’ve rounded up nine that do just that, including a wall desk that puts vertical space to work, a folding desk that collapses to under three inches, and several other petite options that hold their own as furniture, once the laptop is stowed.

Each pick is sized for a compact home office, a bedroom work setup, or an unclaimed nook, and we’ve noted dimensions, materials, and assembly for all of them. If you have a little more room to work with, many have bigger siblings in the same collection.

If you want a standing desk that doesn’t look like office equipment, turn to Article’s Torme. It adjusts its height with a manual lift mechanism hidden in the top, so you can switch between sitting and standing without a motor or a control panel. And while there are no drawers or additional shelves, a few divided compartments are within the desk’s body to keep your daily essentials close by.

For those interested in a work surface that can double as a vanity or living room sideboard, the Sloane Desk from Castlery is up to the task. Elevated by fluted panels and a duo of discreet storage drawers, its look is polished enough to nimbly complement non-office settings. At just over 47 inches wide, it also has room to work as a small computer desk with a monitor, and the drawers keep chargers and paperwork out of sight when it’s off duty.

This Scandi-style desk is all about taking up vertical space—one of our favorite space-saving hacks. Blending metal and wood in one streamlined design, it provides a top shelf for vertical storage and plenty of wall space to add artwork and decor. Choose between a black or white metal frame to match your home’s mood.

A treat for the eyes—and a space-saving solution for any small home—this compact wood desk pairs its clean, design-forward aesthetic with discreet, functional details: a drawer for extra storage, a hidden cubby for cable management, and a fully finished back that allows it to be displayed anywhere in the room. With solid wood legs and slats, it’s a sturdy home office addition that’s both handsome and heirloom-worthy. At $2,495, it’s the priciest pick here. But it’s also the most likely to outlast the apartment it was bought for.

This electric standing desk changes desk height at the touch of a button. At 34 inches wide, it’s the narrowest standing desk on this list. Available in three sizes and color combinations, its ice-blue finish provides a welcome break from ho-hum neutrals—and, if you’d like, you can order a desk chair in the exact same color for a sleek, monochrome setup.

The ultimate small-space-friendly desk is one that folds away when not in use. The Wohomo folding desk collapses to under three inches, so it can slide behind a door or into a closet between workdays. This Amazon option boasts thousands of positive reviews touting its slim profile, and it’s light enough for one person to move on their own. Available in three sizes (31 inches, 39 inches, and 47 inches), you’re able to choose how much workspace you’ll need. This desk is also equipped with a side hook ideal for keeping your headphones, work bag, or jacket off the ground.

When AD featured Emma Chamberlain’s Los Angeles home in 2022, she was already learning the ropes of decorating with the interiors firm Proem. She has since followed that project with a collection for West Elm. The house taught her to weigh function as heavily as looks. “If something looks great but feels bad, it doesn’t make the cut,” she previously told us. The 36-inch mini desk, shown here in dark walnut, is narrow enough for a bedroom corner or the end of a living room, and at $499 it lands in the middle of this list price-wise. If you have the wall for it, the collection also includes a 48-inch storage desk at $899.

What Are the Best Types of Desks for Small Spaces?

For most small spaces, the best options are wall-mounted desks and floating desks, which keep the floor clear and put vertical space to use. Folding desks are another great option since they’re easy to stow away when you’re done for the day. If you have an empty corner space, a corner desk or a small L-shaped desk can claim square footage that otherwise goes unused. A secretary desk, which closes over your work, makes sense if your home office is also your living room.

How Do I Choose the Right Small Computer Desk for My Needs?

The right computer desk for you will depend on your priorities. If you have multiple monitors, choose a desk with a longer shape and a cord organizer to ensure you have enough surface area. If you work on just a laptop, consider a portable C-shaped desk that can double as a side table when not in use. If your desk is too shallow for a keyboard and a screen, a keyboard tray mounted underneath buys you room without a bigger footprint.

While most sitting desks are between 27 and 30 inches tall, your height will play a factor in ergonomics and how comfortable you’ll feel sitting for long periods of time. For example, if you’re taller, you may find yourself slouching to type. For those who are shorter, your neck could be placed at an odd angle for viewing your laptop or monitor screen. If you’d rather keep the desk you already have, a standing desk converter sits on top of it and gives you an adjustable height without adding another piece of furniture.

Branch, a desk and office-chair company that is focused on ergonomics, recommends that the best desk height maintains a 90-degree angle at your elbows when your arms rest on the desk (a.k.a. while you’re typing). Your desk chair will also affect your comfort and ergonomic posture. You can shop our tested and researched picks in our office chairs guide.

What Are the Most Space-Efficient Desks for Small Apartments?

Vertical wall-mounted desks, foldable desks (basically the Murphy bed of workspaces), and C-shaped portable desks are going to be the most space-efficient options for small apartments. Foldable desks can easily be tucked away when not in use, while C-shaped tables can also function as a drinks table after working hours. If you need a space-saving desk with storage, look for vertical storage above the work surface rather than drawers below it, which keeps the desk’s footprint small.

Where Should I Put a Desk in a Small Apartment?

Start with the power outlets. A desk within reach of a wall plug saves you from running extension cords across the room, and it gives you an obvious place for a phone and laptop charging station. After that, look for natural light and a wall or corner that isn’t already doing another job.

#4968. FSB urgest Turkey to tighten crypto oversight, set stablecoin rules

The Financial Stability Board cited progress on crypto licensing and urged stronger enforcement and cooperation with foreign regulators.

The Financial Stability Board (FSB) urged Turkey to tighten its oversight of crypto markets and adopt dedicated rules for stablecoins.

In a peer review published on Wednesday, the global financial watchdog said Turkish authorities had made progress by introducing registration and licensing requirements for crypto service providers.

The report was part of the FSB’s regular program of country reviews and assessed Turkey’s progress in implementing the board’s 2023 global recommendations on crypto and stablecoins. It followed a broader 2025 review that found gaps in how jurisdictions had implemented those recommendations.

The FSB recommended that Turkey begin monitoring crypto-related risks to financial stability under a formal framework, strengthen supervision and enforcement, and improve cooperation and information sharing with foreign regulators.

Turkey’s Capital Markets Board introduced licensing and operational requirements for crypto exchanges and custodians in March 2025, including minimum capital thresholds.

The FSB said Turkey had introduced safeguards including requirements to segregate assets held in custody and a ban on domestic crypto payments. The safeguards help limit the spread of crypto-related risks to banks and payment systems. Authorities also launched a centralized platform to monitor crypto activities.

The FSB’s 2025 review found that Turkey had finalized rules for crypto assets but lacked a dedicated stablecoin framework.

In June 2025, Turkey’s Finance Ministry outlined plans to introduce additional transaction checks and limits on stablecoin transfers to combat money laundering.

Related: Turkey tightens crypto rules with source, purpose checks on transfers

#4967. What Are Solana Privacy Rings? Inside Helius’ Privacy Protocol for Solana

Every transaction on Solana is public by default. Anyone can look up a wallet address and see its balance, what it has sent and received, and when. That transparency is useful for verifying the network, but a problem for anyone who doesn't want their finances on display: a business paying salaries, a trader building a position, or an individual who simply doesn't want strangers seeing their savings.

Rings are programmable, onchain environments for encrypting money, markets, and finance - without ever leaving Solana

Now live on mainnet. pic.twitter.com/ezCIUCjcbI

Rings support SOL and other tokens on Solana, including SPL and Token-2022 tokens. Users keep custody of their funds throughout, since every transaction needs the owner's signature.

Crucially, the private balances live on the same network as everything else. Private transactions execute directly on Solana and encrypted data is stored on Solana's ledger, with no separate layer-2 network or sequencer in between. That means private balances sit alongside the same stablecoins, tokenized assets and DeFi liquidity as public ones.

Each private transfer comes with a zero-knowledge proof, a piece of cryptography that lets the network confirm the transfer is valid, for example that the sender actually had the funds, without revealing the numbers involved. Rings use a type of zero-knowledge proof called a Groth16 zk-SNARK, which Helius says can be generated in tens of milliseconds, either by a service provider or on the user's own device. Each private transfer settles in a single Solana transaction.

Users read their own encrypted balances with a viewing key. They can decrypt their balance locally, or let a service provider decrypt it and serve it to them, and they can share a key for a single transaction with an auditor or counterparty without exposing the rest of their history.

Moving funds into a Ring from an ordinary public wallet, or back out again, is visible onchain, including the asset and amount. But users who on-ramp from fiat currency straight into a private balance, through a supported provider, keep the asset and amount hidden from the start, according to Helius.

Because Custom Rings interoperate with the default Ring, regulated firms can keep their activity confidential from the market while still sharing the same liquidity as everyone else.

Private transfers are just the start. Solana programs can hold encrypted balances in private escrows and release them when set conditions are met, opening the way for:

At launch, Decrypt, mining and staking app ORE and cross-chain service Aurora Intents were using the default Ring, while Backpack, DFlow, Jupiter, Squads and Titan were among the teams working on integrations.

Helius says the core programs and zero-knowledge circuits have been audited by security firms Certora, OtterSec and ZK Security. Engineers from the Solana Foundation, Blueshift, Zcash and other teams took part in the "trusted setup," the one-off ceremony that generates the parameters the proofs rely on.

The code is open source, published on GitHub under the Apache 2.0 license, so anyone can inspect how it works.

Formal verification, a mathematical check that the circuits behave exactly as intended, is still underway and expected to finish by the end of 2026. Solana's own documentation currently describes Rings as being in beta.

#4962. Bitcoin Covenants Part 5: MATT and OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY — How Bitcoin could carry state across transactions

OP_CCV embeds contract state directly into Taproot output keys and enforces valid state transitions onchain. This makes it possible to achieve stateful contracts and compact fraud proofs.

This is Part 5 in the technical article series about Bitcoin covenants by Cointelegraph Research. To read the previous article click here.

MATT (Merkleize All The Things) is a smart contract framework for Bitcoin that enables stateful contracts and scalable fraud proofs. A stateful contract carries information forward across multiple transactions and can update this information as the contract evolves. Standard Bitcoin scripts do not provide this capability, because each transaction is evaluated independently with no memory of previous executions.

Every covenant proposal allows state-carrying UTXOs to an extent, as illustrated by scripting languages such as Sapio, which builds on OP_CTV. This works by using addresses to represent a finite state automaton, where transactions function as the state transitions. However, there is no way to elegantly implement numerical logic in this paradigm, a gap filled by MATT, and in particular its proposed implementation through OP_CCV. Because MATT allows committed data to be computed at spend time and re-embedded into the next output, each spend can derive a new state dynamically, instead of choosing from a predetermined set of states.

To understand the MATT proposal, it is useful to first review how Taproot commits data through public keys. Every Taproot output contains a base public key, called the internal key, which can be cryptographically modified or “tweaked” to commit to extra information. Formally, the tweaked public key is Q = P + t·G, where P is the internal key, G is the generator of the secp256k1 elliptic curve, and t is a tagged hash of P and the committed data.

In a regular Taproot output, this commitment typically encodes the Merkle root of a taptree, which contains hidden spending paths. MATT builds on this mechanism to also embed contract state into the key. Instead of embedding complex logic directly in Bitcoin’s scripting language, MATT encodes computation as a sequence of state commitments, and the chain verifies only that each state transition is valid.

MATT can be realized through different opcode combinations. The most direct and well-specified implementation relies on OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY (OP_CCV). OP_CCV provides the primitive that makes state-carrying UTXOs feasible within Taproot.  OP_CCV allows a script to verify that a Taproot input or output key has been tweaked to commit to a specific piece of state data. The internal key is first tweaked with a hash of the contract’s current state, and then Taproot’s standard taptree tweak is applied on top. The resulting output key therefore commits to both the tapscript  (the set of spending conditions) and the current state of the contract.

OP_CCV can then enforce what state is carried forward into the next output. It allows the script to inspect the state data provided on the witness stack, perform computations on it, and require that the resulting updated state is correctly re-committed in a specified output. This is how state transitions are enforced on-chain. The stack format is:

OP_CHECKCONTRACTVERIFY

The opcode supports three modes for checking an output as well as a separate input mode. In default mode, the entire BTC value of the input UTXO is allocated to the specified output; in deduct mode, only a portion equal to the output’s amount is assigned, leaving the remainder available for further outputs; and in ignore mode, the script and data commitment are verified without constraining the amount.  Because default and deduct modes are designed to preserve value across checked outputs, transaction fees must be paid through separate fee-paying inputs. A dedicated fee input and change output can be added to the transaction without conflicting with the covenant’s constraints. This prevents the accidental burning of funds that can occur in more primitive covenant implementations, such as OP_CTV or SIGHASH_ANYPREVOUT. It also addresses a major limitation of presigned-transaction-style covenants, which typically define amounts upfront and therefore cannot accommodate dynamic fee adjustments or partial unvaults.

When OP_CCV is combined with OP_CAT, the full MATT framework becomes even more expressive. OP_CAT enables byte concatenation in Script, which makes it possible to construct and verify Merkle proofs directly on-chain. Two distinct Merkle trees are associated with a Taproot output in such an arrangement. The first is the familiar Taproot script tree (the taptree), whose leaves are the contract’s spending scripts. The second is a separate state tree, whose leaves are the contract’s data, such as the intermediate steps of a computation.  The OP_CCV + OP_CAT script can verify a Merkle proof against the current state tree root, compute the updated root after a transition, and use OP_CCV to force the next output to commit to it.

This construction enables fraud-proof systems with a slashing condition in which a long computation is committed off-chain, and only the specific disputed step (with its path in the binary tree) needs to be revealed and verified on-chain. Similar systems exist as optimistic rollups on other blockchain systems: the full computation runs off-chain, and the on-chain contract only intervenes when someone raises a challenge. Fraud proofs require that the computation be compiled down to a binary circuit or equivalent step-by-step representation that can be committed to in a Merkle tree. OP_CCV + OP_CAT would avoid the need for the Lamport one-time signatures and pre-signed transaction graphs that BitVM relies on to enforce state consistency.

Beyond fraud proofs, the OP_CCV and OP_CAT combination also has implications for vaults. OP_CCV alone can implement vaults, but when combined with OP_CAT, it allows fully featured vaults that are essentially equivalent to the functionality proposed by OP_VAULT. As James O’Beirne, the author of OP_VAULT, has noted, OP_CCV represents a more general and robust design that effectively supersedes his earlier proposal.

In the next article in this series, we will move on from general covenant proposals to review application-specific opcodes such as OP_VAULT, OP_UNVAULT, and OP_EVICT. Each of the opcodes are designed to solve a specific problem rather than provide broad programmability.

Magazine: Bitcoin holders have received eCash — What can you do with it?

#4960. Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin Faces First Back-to-Back Annual Losses in History, But There's a Catch

Bitcoin is flirting with a historical first that bulls probably don’t want to hear: Two consecutive years of losses, something the cryptocurrency has never experienced in its 18-year history.

The world's largest cryptocurrency finished 2025 down 6.34% and has lost another 15.65% so far in 2026, trading around $73,850 on November 10. If Bitcoin fails to recover above its annual opening price of roughly $87,500 before December 31, it will close two calendar years in the red for the first time.

Historical annual returns show Bitcoin has experienced several brutal crashes since its creation, but each negative calendar year has been followed by a positive one. Even the disastrous 2018 and 2022 bear markets failed to produce consecutive annual losses.

The historical pattern is clear. Its significance, however, deserves a closer look.

Bitcoin's previous annual crashes were considerably more painful than its current losing streak. The cryptocurrency plunged 73.56% in 2018 and another 64.27% in 2022, wiping out most of investors' holdings in each respective year.

By comparison, Bitcoin's 6.34% decline in 2025 was relatively mild. Add the roughly 16% loss recorded this year, and the combined decline comes to approximately 21% after accounting for compounding.

That's a fraction of the losses investors endured in previous crypto winters, despite the market potentially recording a longer sequence of negative annual returns.

Bitcoin's performance in 2024 also provides some perspective. The cryptocurrency surged more than 120% that year, meaning investors who bought at the beginning of 2024 would still be sitting on substantial gains despite the subsequent decline.

The picture changes considerably when measuring losses from the market's actual peak instead of calendar-year boundaries.

Bitcoin reached approximately $126,000 in October 2025 before crashing below $58,000 during 2026. That represented a decline exceeding 50%, a significantly more painful move than the 21% combined loss reflected in the two annual candlesticks.

Analysts have pointed to Bitcoin's increasingly institutional investor base, spot exchange-traded funds, and deeper market liquidity as potential explanations for its comparatively smaller bear-market drawdowns.

Those structural changes may help explain why Bitcoin's current market cycle looks different from previous ones, although they have not eliminated major price crashes.

There is also a distinction between the length of a losing streak and the severity of the losses. A market can decline slowly over several calendar years without inflicting the same damage as a single catastrophic year.

And Bitcoin has already demonstrated that a bad year doesn't necessarily translate into a prolonged downturn. The cryptocurrency gained roughly 92% in 2019 following its 2018 collapse and surged more than 155% in 2023 after its disastrous 2022 performance.

For now, Bitcoin has 50 days left in 2026 to avoid its first back-to-back annual losses. A third consecutive decline in 2027 would extend the streak further, but that scenario is still hypothetical.

At its November 10 price of approximately $73,850, Bitcoin would need to gain about 18.5% to recover its 2026 opening price and finish the year in positive territory.
